Lower bounding ground-state energies of local Hamiltonians through the renormalization group
Ilya Kull, Norbert Schuch, Ben Dive, Miguel Navascués

Abstract
Given a renormalization scheme, we show how to formulate a tractable convex relaxation of the set of feasible local density matrices of a many-body quantum system. The relaxation is obtained by introducing a hierarchy of constraints between the reduced states of ever-growing sets of lattice sites. The coarse-graining maps of the underlying renormalization procedure serve to eliminate a vast number of those constraints, such that the remaining ones can be enforced with reasonable computational means. This can be used to obtain rigorous lower bounds on the ground state energy of arbitrary local Hamiltonians, by performing a linear optimization over the resulting convex relaxation of reduced quantum states. The quality of the bounds crucially depends on the particular renormalization scheme, which must be tailored to the target Hamiltonian. We apply our method to 1D translation-invariant spin models, obtaining energy bounds comparable to those attained by optimizing over locally translation-invariant states of n ≳ 100 n\gtrsim 100 spins. Beyond this demonstration, the general method can be applied to a wide range of other problems, such as spin systems in higher spatial dimensions, electronic structure problems, and various other many-body optimization problems, such as entanglement and nonlocality detection.



I
Introduction

Studying the properties of quantum many-body systems presents great computational challenges.
The vastness of the space of parameters describing states of such systems prohibits the direct computation of properties of interest in all but a few cases. The paradigmatic problem in this regard is finding the ground state energy of a system, i.e., the minimum eigenvalue of its Hamiltonian operator.
A well-known paradigm for solving such problems approximately is the variational-ansatz approach.
In this approach the wavefunction is parametrized in terms of fewer variables than would be needed to describe all possible quantum states of the system.
Minimizing the energy with respect to those parameters then leads to an
upper bound
on the ground state energy.
However, in almost all cases rigorous error bounds on the obtained upper bound—that is, its distance from the true value—are lacking. Thus, additional methods will be needed to certify the obtained solution, such as to obtain rigorous bounds on the true value.

A complementary approximation paradigm which can provide such certificates is the
relaxation
approach.
Here, the optimization problem is simplified by omitting some of the constraints that appear in the exact problem,
thus resulting in a
lower bound
.
An example of this approach is the so-called reduced-density-matrix theory
Mazziotti and Rice 2007
;
Nakata et al. 2001
;
Baumgratz and Plenio 2012
:
It uses that for local Hamiltonians, the energy only depends on few-particle reduced states, and one can thus optimize over those reduced states rather than global wavefunctions
Coulson 1960
;
Coleman 1963
;
Mayer 1955
;
Löwdin 1955
. However, in order to obtain the true ground state energy one has to ensure that the reduced states are compatible with a global wavefunction. This problem is known as the quantum marginal problem (for spins)

Yu et al. 2021
;
Walter et al. 2013
;
Aloy et al. 2021
;
Kim 2021
;
Klyachko 2006

or
N
-representability problem (for electrons)
Klyachko 2006
;
Mazziotti and Rice 2007
. Perhaps unsurprisingly, this problem is no easier than the original ground state problem

Aharonov et al. 2009
;
Gottesman and Irani 2009
;
Bausch et al. 2017
;
Liu 2006
;
Broadbent and Grilo 2020
;
Liu et al. 2007
. In order to obtain a tractable problem,
one can now relax the marginal problem by expressing consistency with a global wavefunction as a hierarchy of consistency
conditions, and only keeping the most relevant of those constraints. Dropping constraints enlarges the space of reduced
states over which the minimization is carried out, and thus gives rise to
lower bounds
on the ground state energy.
Crucially, this minimization is a semidefinite program (SDP)
Vandenberghe and Boyd 1996
and can therefore be solved efficiently and exactly, providing rigorous lower bounds.

Variational-ansatz methods are predominant in the modern-day computational toolbox.
The variety of methods and their success can be attributed to the possibility of fine tuning the ansatz in order to address a specific Hamiltonian.
Moreover, in many notable cases, physical insight has guided the construction of simple ansatz families which are capable of capturing the relevant structure of the system under consideration exactly—examples range from BCS theory over Laughlin or Gutzwiller projected wavefunctions all the way to tensor network ansatzes.

Relaxation methods, on the other hand, are far less diverse and less tunable: relaxations are typically formulated by first deriving an exhaustive hierarchy of constraints, and then
discarding all constraints that exceed a certain level of complexity (e.g. as in Refs.
Mazziotti 2006
;
Baumgratz and Plenio 2012
;
Navascués et al. 2007
;
Doherty et al. 2004
;
Haim et al. 2020
).
Since the complexity within the hierarchy generally grows exponentially, typically all but
the lowest-level constraints have to be dropped.
While for some systems, even few constraints can already produce useful lower bounds
Baumgratz and Plenio 2012
;
Mazziotti and Rice 2007
,
and cleverly selecting which constraints to keep from the different levels of the hierarchy can lead to improvements

Barthel and Hübener 2012
;
Requena et al. 2021
;
Han 2020
,
the level of precision attainable by relaxation methods is still
rather far away from what can be achieved with state-of-the-art variational algorithms.

In this paper, we propose a general framework to construct relaxations
of optimization problems for quantum many-body systems, such as the ground
state problem. Our method yields a rich and adjustable family of relaxations
which can be continuously tuned and adapted to the specific scenario. Its
construction is guided by renormalization, which allows to use the established knowledge
on renormalization transformations of quantum systems to closely adapt the relaxation
to the problem at hand.
More generally, our framework can be seen as a comprehensive way to
efficiently and systematically relax sets of local many-body
correlations with specific global consistency conditions, and is thus applicable to a wide range of problems,
including tasks such as entanglement or non-locality detection.

The key idea of our method is as follows. We start by expanding the
compatibility constraints on the physical reduced density matrices as a
hierarchy of increasingly complex objects. However, unlike previous approaches, which
truncate
this hierarchy at some level
n
, we first

compress
(or “coarse-grain”) the objects in the hierarchy, such
that each of them becomes of constant (or otherwise tractable) size. This way,
we circumvent the exponential growth of complexity with the level
n
in the
hierarchy, which allows us to reach significantly larger values of
n
. The
attainable accuracy is thus no longer limited by
n
, but rather by the
chosen compression scheme, including the degree of compression. Unlike the
static truncation at fixed
n
, this compression can
now be adjusted continuously, and it can be tuned to the problem at hand,
guided e.g. by renormalization ideas. This way, the relaxation can be
adjusted to the specific scenario, and much higher accuracies can be
obtained.

We present a detailed realization of our method for the central problem of
finding the ground state energy density of a one-dimensional (1D) spin
chain. We show that in this case, both Matrix Product States (MPS) and
Tree Tensor Networks (TTN) provide suitable coarse-graining schemes,
corresponding to Wilson’s NRG and real-space renormalization, respectively

Cirac and Verstraete 2009
.
We test the method on a range of paradigmatic spin
chains, both critical and gapped. We find that our relaxation method
yields lower bounds which are between one and two orders of magnitude
better than what could be reached with existing approaches which truncate
the hierarchy without compression, equivalent to exactly solving systems on the
order of
100
spins.
We find renormalization to be a useful guide for adjusting the coarse-graining scheme to the target Hamiltonian,
that is, that using MPS or TTN describing variationally optimized wavefunctions for the compression in our relaxation, performs indeed rather well. Yet, we also demonstrate that
further optimizing over those coarse-graining maps can additionally
enhance the accuracy of the method.
Our results provide rigorous lower bounds on the energy densities
of infinite spin chains, and significantly improve upon lower bounds
previously obtained for such systems using reduced-density-matrix
theory and similar approaches
Baumgratz and Plenio 2012
;
Barthel and Hübener 2012
;
Haim et al. 2020
.

The method presented can be straightforwardly generalized to ground state
problems in higher dimensions, different geometries, or fermionic systems, by
choosing a suitable renormalization scheme and the corresponding tensor network ansatz.
The general framework underlying the method is much broader, and can be
adapted to handle a diverse range of problems, ranging from electronic
structure problems in condensed matter and quantum chemistry all the way
to entanglement
Peres 1996
;
Horodecki et al. 1996
;
Gurvits 2003

and nonlocality detection

Tura et al. 2014
;
Tura et al. 2017
;
Fadel and Tura 2017
;
Baccari et al. 2019
;
Frérot and Roscilde 2021
in
many-body systems, as well as other situations where the positivity of
intractably large matrices, subject to semidefinite constraints, has to be ensured
Lin 2020
;
Han et al. 2020
;
Berenstein and Hulsey 2021
.

The presentation of the material in the paper aims to equally bridge two
aspects: On the one hand, a comprehensive presentation of the general
method, and on the other hand, a tangible discussion of its
application to 1D spin systems.
In order to enhance the accessibility of the manuscript to the
reader, we have thus structured it as follows:

•

Section

II
provides a
concise summary of the
method
: It derives the method specifically for the case of 1D spin chains
with MPS as coarse-graining maps,
and provides a brief summary of the central numerical findings. It is
self-contained
and can be read as an “executive summary”
of our work. After having read
Section

II
,
the reader should be able to continue directly with those sections which interest them the most.

•

Section

III
introduces the framework in full generality. This
general formulation is set up such that it can be particularized to a wide range of
scenarios, as well as to various underlying renormalization schemes.

•

Section

IV
particularizes the method to 1D spin chains; beyond the
material covered in
Section

II
(rephrased in the general formalism), it also
covers TTN as coarse-graining maps.

•

Section

V
discusses specialized aspects, namely how to optimize
coarse-grainers and how to certify solutions.

•

Section

VI
reports our numerical findings for 1D spin chains, and
discusses the results and the methods used in detail.

•

Section

VII
discusses how to apply the general framework to a range
of other scenarios, such as reduced-density-matrix theory or non-locality
and entanglement detection.

Finally, in
Section

VIII
we present our conclusions.

II
A case study: 1D translation-invariant Hamiltonians

In this section, we will introduce our method and illustrate its key ideas by means of a concrete problem.
Specifically, we will consider the paradigmatic
problem of finding the minimum energy density of a translation-invariant local Hamiltonian
on a one-dimensional (1D) spin chain, also known as the 1D translation-invariant local Hamiltonian problem.
We will see that in this scenario, a natural choice of coarse-graining maps to relax the problem will be given by translation-invariant matrix product states (MPS). Remarkably, the same MPS that have been variationally optimized to upper bound the ground state energy turn out to perform very well as coarse-graining maps for the lower bound as well. We conclude the section by providing numerical results which demonstrate the significant improvement in accuracy that can be obtained through our method.

II.1
Setting Up the Problem as a Hierarchy of Constraints

We start by rephrasing the ground state energy problem as a hierarchy of semidefinite constraints.
The problem that we wish to solve is finding the minimum energy density of a translation-invariant Hamiltonian consisting of an identical nearest-neighbors interaction term
h
, acting on every pair of consecutive spins on an infinite chain.
As the Hamiltonian is translation invariant, we can restrict our minimization to states that have the same symmetry.
Because all the interaction terms are identical, the energy density can be evaluated in terms of the 2-body reduced density matrix,
\rho^{(2)}
. To obtain the true ground state energy density of the infinite system we need to restrict
\rho^{(2)}
to be compatible with a global translation-invariant (TI) state. We formally represent this condition by
\rho^{(2)}\leftarrow\psi_{\text{TI}}
.
(We do not elaborate on what it means to be a state on the infinite chain as from now on we will only use rather obvious properties of the reduced states of
\psi_{\text{TI}}
on finite regions.)
With this notation the problem reads

\displaystyle E_{\text{TI}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min_{\rho^{(2)},\psi_{\text{TI}}}

\displaystyle\mbox{Tr}\left(h\rho^{(2)}\right)

(1)

s.t.

\displaystyle\rho^{(2)}\geq 0,\;\mbox{Tr}(\rho^{(2)})=1\;,

\displaystyle\rho^{(2)}\leftarrow\psi_{\text{TI}}\;.

The existence of a global state
\psi_{\text{TI}}
implies that all its
m
-body reduced states
\rho^{(m)}
also exist and are compatible with each other in the sense that
\rho^{(m-1)}
is obtained from
\rho^{(m)}
by tracing out one spin.
Since
\psi_{\text{TI}}
is assumed to be translation invariant, tracing out the leftmost spin gives the same reduced state as tracing out the rightmost one.
We denote this compatibility condition as

\rho^{(m-1)}\leftarrow\rho^{(m)}\hskip 10.0pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 10.0pt\rho^{(m-1)}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{L}}(\rho^{(m)})=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{R}}(\rho^{(m)})\;,

(2)

where
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{L}}
denotes the partial trace of the leftmost spin (and similarly
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{R}}
).
We call a state
\rho^{(m)}
with this property
locally translation invariant
(LTI):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{L}}(\rho^{(m)})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{R}}(\rho^{(m)})

(The LTI condition.)

(3)

We now rewrite
Eq.

1
by explicitly including the compatibility conditions between the reduced states
\rho^{(m)}
of
\psi_{\text{TI}}
for
m\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
in the constraints as follows

\begin{split}E_{\text{TI}}=\min_{\{\rho^{(m)}\},\psi_{\text{TI}}}&\mbox{Tr}\left(h\rho^{(2)}\right)\\[2.0pt]
\mbox{s.t. }&\mbox{Tr}(\rho^{(2)})=1,\;\rho^{(m)}\geq 0\mbox{ for all }m\in\{2,\ldots,n\}\;,\\
&\rho^{(2)}\leftarrow\rho^{(3)}\leftarrow\rho^{(4)}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow\rho^{(n-1)}\leftarrow\rho^{(n)}\leftarrow\psi_{\text{TI}}\;.\end{split}

(4)

II.2
First Relaxation: Truncating the Hierarchy

Next we relax
Eq.

4
by dropping the condition requiring the existence of
\psi_{\text{TI}}
but keeping all the other constraints. That is, we require that the reduced states
\rho^{(m)}
for
m=\{2,\ldots,n\}
exist and are compatible in the sense of
Eq.

2
. In particular this implies that
\rho^{(n)}
is LTI.
We refer to the resulting problem as the LTI problem of size
n
and its solution as
E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
:

\begin{split}E_{\text{LTI}}(n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min_{\{\rho^{(m)}\}}&\mbox{Tr}\left(h\rho^{(2)}\right)\\[2.0pt]
\mbox{s.t. }&\mbox{Tr}(\rho^{(2)})=1\;,\\[2.0pt]
&\rho^{(m)}\geq 0\;,\mbox{ for all }m\in\{2,\ldots,n\}\;,\\
&\rho^{(m-1)}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{L}}(\rho^{(m)})=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{R}}(\rho^{(m)})\;,\mbox{ for all }m\in\{3,\ldots,n\}\;.\end{split}

(5)

By removing the constraint

\rho^{(n)}\leftarrow\psi_{\text{TI}}

we allow more states
\{\rho^{(m)}\}
to be considered in the minimization and therefore obtain a lower bound, i.e.
E_{\text{TI}}\geq E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
for all
n\geq 2
.
The resulting optimization problem,
Eq.

5
, involves matrices of finite sizes and can be solved numerically using semidefinite programming (SDP)
Vandenberghe and Boyd 1996
.
For an LTI state
\rho^{(n+1)}
,
Eq.

3
implies that
\rho^{(n)}
is also LTI, we therefore have that the sequence
\{E_{\text{LTI}}(n)\}_{n}
is non-decreasing.
Furthermore, it converges to the exact energy density in the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
.

The analysis so far suggests the following strategy for solving
Eq.

1
: Solve
Eq.

5
while keeping as many states
\rho^{(m)}
as we can fit in memory, up to some
m_{\text{max}}
.
Unfortunately, this approach does not lead to accurate lower bounds because the resources needed to solve the resulting SDP scale exponentially with
m_{\text{max}}
.

II.3
Second Relaxation: Applying Coarse-Graining to the Constraints

So far we have shown that the LTI problem,
Eq.

5
, is a relaxation of the translation-invariant local Hamiltonian problem,
Eq.

1
.
We also pointed out that to obtain tight lower bounds one has to solve the LTI problem for
n\gg 1
, which is not a tractable task due to the exponentially large state
\rho^{(n)}
appearing in
Eq.

5
.
To overcome this impasse we suggest to relax the hierarchy of constraints appearing in the LTI problem, which we have formally written as

\rho^{(2)}\leftarrow\rho^{(3)}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow\rho^{(n)}
,
in a smarter way than just truncating it at a low level
m_{\text{max}}\ll n
.
In particular, we would like to keep
some
of the constraints arising due to the state
\rho^{(n)}
rather than discard it completely.
To achieve this we apply a renormalization procedure to the states
\rho^{(m)}
.
We now explain how one constraint of the form
\rho^{(m-1)}\leftarrow\rho^{(m)}
can be relaxed and compressed by applying a coarse-graining map.
In the next subsection we explain how this can be iterated to relax and compress the entire LTI problem,
Eq.

5
.

To explain the following steps we rely on graphical tensor notation, in which the constraints
\rho^{(m-1)}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{L}}(\rho^{(m)})=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{R}}(\rho^{(m)})
appearing in
Eq.

5
are written as follows (we focus on
m=3,4,5
):

\begin{split}\hbox to33.18pt{\vbox to29.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 27.97008pt\lower-14.99532pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\hbox to33.18pt{\vbox to29.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 27.97008pt\lower-14.99532pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(6)

where the partial trace is represented by connecting the corresponding legs of the tensor.

Now consider the first two rows in
Eq.

6
. The following steps form the elementary building block of our procedure and are described in
Eq.

7
.
First we apply to both sides of each equation a coarse-graining map
W_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{\chi}
, which maps two spins of dimension
d
into one new site of dimension
\chi
, such that in both rows the two central spins of
\rho^{(4)}
are acted upon, leading to the equations in the center of
Eq.

7
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(7)

(In the diagrams
d
is indicated by thin lines and
\chi
by thick ones.)
Next, we can
compress
the coarse-grained relations, as described by the second arrow in
Eq.

7
:
We notice that after the coarse-graining,
\rho^{(3)}
is related only to the image of
\rho^{(4)}
under the coarse-graining map
C_{2}(\rho^{(4)})=({\mathbb{I}}\otimes W_{2}\otimes{\mathbb{I}})\rho^{(4)}({\mathbb{I}}\otimes W^{\dagger}_{2}\otimes{\mathbb{I}})
.
We can therefore encode the coarse-grained constraints using a smaller-dimensional variable
\omega^{(4)}
.

What we have achieved is to formulate a relaxation of the constraints on
\rho^{(3)}
in terms of a smaller variable. In the example we just described, the size reduction was not so significant because we only coarse-grained two spins. However, by iterating the above coarse-graining and compression steps as we will now explain, we can drastically reduce the overall size of the problem.

II.4
Iterative Coarse-Graining: a Renormalization Procedure

The next step is to iterate the above described coarse-graining and compression in order to coarse-grain all the states
\rho^{(m)}
up to
m=n
for large values of
n
.
To do so we require the coarse-graining maps to satisfy certain compatibility conditions that we shall now explain.
Consider the constraints involving
\rho^{(4)}
and
\rho^{(5)}
(the last two rows in
Eq.

6
).
In order to compress
\rho^{(5)}
we need to apply a 3-body coarse-graining map
W_{3}
. We would like to have the following:

(8)

However, we notice that we may no longer write conditions involving
\rho^{(4)}
as we have already compressed it and replaced it with
\omega^{(4)}
when we treated the first two constraints.
In order to continue we thus need to be able to express the left-hand side of
Eq.

8
in terms of
\omega^{(4)}
which appears in
Eq.

7
. This can be done if the coarse-graining maps
W_{3}
and
W_{2}
are related as follows.

(9)

i.e. we require the existence of two additional coarse-graining maps,
L_{2}
and
R_{2}
, that act on the output of
W_{2}
together with an additional spin on the left or right side respectively.
Given maps that satisfy
Eq.

9
we can substitute
W_{3}
in
Eq.

8
with the appropriate expression, such that
W_{2}
acts on the two central spins in
\rho^{(4)}
, as shown in
Eq.

10
.
Then the left-hand side of
Eq.

8
can be expressed in terms of
\omega^{(4)}
and
L_{2}
(respectively
R_{2}
), and the right-hand side—in terms of
\omega^{(5)}
:

(10)

We can continue in this fashion and coarse-grain all the states up to
\rho^{(n)}
if for all
m=2,\ldots,n-3
we have (analogously to
Eq.

9
)

W_{m+1}=R_{m}\circ(W_{m}\otimes{\mathbb{I}})=L_{m}\circ({\mathbb{I}}\otimes W_{m})\;.

(11)

II.5
An Ansatz for the Coarse-Graining Maps: Matrix Product States

We have just shown that we can coarse-grain and compress all the states
\rho^{(m)}
appearing in
Eq.

5
consistently if the coarse-graining maps satisfy
Eq.

11
.
There is a simple way to satisfy
Eq.

11
: Uniform matrix-product states (MPS) are translation-invariant states encoded by a single rank-3 tensor with dimensions
(D,d,D)
where
d
is the dimension of each spin and
D
is the so-called bond dimension (or virtual dimension). Contracting
m
copies of the tensor along the bonds results in a map from
m
spins to the bonds at the edges.
Representing the MPS tensor by a circle and the bond indices by red lines, the maps
W_{m}
are defined as follows (e.g. for
m=3
)

\hbox to43.08pt{\vbox to23.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.76595pt\lower 5.49046pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -39.37 15.75 L -39.37 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.69 15.75 L -19.69 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 15.75 L 0 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -21.65 15.75 L -21.65 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}

(12)

In this case the coarse-graining dimension
\chi
equals
D^{2}
.
The maps
L_{m}
(and respectively
R_{m}
) are then chosen to be the same for all
m
and act by contracting an additional MPS tensor from the left (respectively from the right) and as the identity on the other bond.

With this choice of maps, we apply the above described steps to the LTI problem,
Eq.

5
, to obtain the following (shown up to
m
=5):

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.8}{0.0}{0.0}{0.8}{-23.85608pt}{-3.55157pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}
{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.\end{split}

(13)

For
m>5
the last two constraints on the right side of
Eq.

13
are repeated between the pairs of states
\omega^{(m-1)},\omega^{(m)}
for
m=6,\ldots,n
.
The final relaxation is then obtained by replacing the constraints in
Eq.

5
starting from
m=3
by the ones in
Eq.

13
and in addition demanding
\omega^{(m)}\geq 0
for
m=4,\ldots,n
.
(For the full details refer to
Eq.

35
in
Section

IV
below.)
Every state
\omega^{(m)}
has dimensions
d^{2}D^{2}\times d^{2}D^{2}
. The memory scaling of the relaxation is therefore
O(nd^{4}D^{4})
.

The error of the lower bound obtained with the relaxed constraints
Eq.

13
depends on the MPS tensor used to perform the coarse-graining.
In the resulting SDP, the entries of the MPS tensor appear as parameters which can be optimized in order to tighten the lower bound.
The bond dimension
D
determines the dimension of the subspace to which the original constraints are restricted due to the action of the coarse-graining maps. Increasing
D
allows to keep more constraints.

Viewing MPS as describing coarse-graining maps is by no means a new insight. This idea lies at the heart of the density matrix renormalization group algorithm
White 1992
;
Schollwöck 2011
where the MPS produced at each iteration serves to coarse-grain the Hamiltonian in the next one.
This connection suggests that an MPS approximation to the ground state, when used in our scheme, might give rise to a tight relaxation for the same Hamiltonian.

Following this reasoning, in our implementation we used the tensor describing the MPS ground-state approximation obtained from a variational algorithm based on a uniform-MPS ansatz—the VUMPS algorithm
Zauner-Stauber et al. 2018
.
The results which we will now present demonstrate that this heuristic choice performed very well. (For further analysis of the choice of MPS tensor for the relaxation refer to
Sections

III.4
and
VI.4
below.)

II.6
Numerical Results

Let us now demonstrate the power of the method through the numerical study of some selected spin models. The models presented here serve to demonstrate the general performance of the method within the framework of this introductory section; results on a wider range of models, together with a more detailed discussion of the numerical findings and an analysis of the performance of the method, can be found in
Section

VI
.

In the following, we present results obtained for two paradigmatic spin
1/2
models: the critical transverse-field Ising (TFI) model, and the isotropic antiferromagnetic Heisenberg chain.
For each model, we solved the relaxation described
in
Eq.

13
for various values of
n
and bond dimensions
D
.
We then determined the deviation

\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}(n,D)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{\text{TI}}-{E}^{\text{relax.}}(n,D)\geq 0

of the obtained lower bound
E^{\mathrm{relax.}}(n,D)
from the exact ground state energy density
E_{\mathrm{TI}}
.
For comparison, we also computed the LTI energy density
E_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}
of the exact hierarchy truncated at the
n
-th level, by solving
Eq.

5
for attainable values of
n
, and the corresponding deviation

\Delta{E}_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{\text{TI}}-E_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}
from the exact value.
Since our method is a relaxation of the LTI problem
Eq.

5
, it holds that

\Delta{E}_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}\leq\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}(n,D)
for all
D
.

Figure

1
shows
\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}(n,D)
for different values of
D
(colored dashed and dash-dotted lines), as well as
\Delta{E}_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}
(black line with circles) as a function of
n
for the TFI model (panel
a
) and the Heisenberg model (panel
b
).
We observe that the accuracy obtained from the exact hierarchy truncated at
level
n
,
\Delta{E}_{\text{LTI}}
, displays an algebraic decay
n^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\approx 2
; however, one is limited to rather small
n
and thus low accuracies. The curves obtained from our relaxation initially follow the same curve as
\Delta E_{\text{LTI}}
and in fact extrapolate it to larger
n
, and as
n
is increased eventually saturate at some
\Delta E^{\text{relax.}}(n=\infty,D)
. It is immediately evident from the plots that the accuracy obtained from our relaxation is between one and two orders of magnitude higher than that obtained from the LTI hierarchy. From the plots, we can estimate the effective
n\equiv n_{\text{eff.}}(D)
required to reach the same accuracy from the exact LTI hierarchy (by linear extrapolation of the latter in the log-log plot); we find

n_{\text{eff.}}(6)\simeq 120
for the critical TFI model, and
n_{\text{eff.}}(7)\simeq 60
for the Heisenberg antiferromagnet, both significantly beyond what can be achieved without the relaxation.

Figure 1:

Distance
\Delta E^{\text{relax.}}
of the lower bounds to the ground state energy density,
obtained by solving the MPS-based relaxation outlined in
Section

II
, to the true value,
for (a) the transverse field Ising model at the critical point, and (b) the spin
1/2
Heisenberg antiferromagnet.
The differently colored dashed and dash-dotted lines show results obtained using MPS with different bond dimensions
D
(leading to better relaxations). The black circles give the energy obtained by solving the exact hierarchy up to
n
sites, whose computational cost grows exponentially in
n
; it displays an algebraic scaling. The MPS relaxations approximate its behavior increasingly well as
D
increases, at a fraction of the computation cost (which scales algebraically in the effective
n
), thus allowing to reach accuracies of between one and two orders of magnitude higher.

The same features were observed in all other models on which we tested our method; in fact, for gapped models, the observed convergence was even more rapid. For an overview of all results and a detailed discussion of the various findings and numerical methods, we refer the reader to
Section

VI
.

III
The general method

We will now describe our relaxation method in a general setting.
In order to do so we begin by setting up the language needed to describe general locality scenarios.

III.1
Background: the Quantum Marginal Problem

Let
{\cal A}=\{a,b,c,\ldots\}
be a finite or infinite alphabet where each letter
a\in{\cal A}
represents a physical system, with Hilbert space
{\cal H}_{a}
of dimension
d_{a}
.
For a given collection of sets of letters
{\cal I}\subset 2^{\cal A}
, we consider the ensemble of quantum states
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
, where each
\rho_{I}
acts on
{\cal H}_{I}=\bigotimes_{a\in I}{\cal H}_{a}
. The set
{\cal I}
will be said to define our
marginal scenario
.

The
quantum marginal problem
consists of finding whether for a given state ensemble
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
there exists an overall quantum state
\sigma
on
{\cal H}_{{\cal A}}
such that
\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal A}\setminus I}(\sigma)=\rho_{I}
for all
I\in{\cal I}
. In that case, we will say that the ensemble
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I}
admits a
global state dilation

\sigma
. Of course, a necessary condition for
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
to admit an overall dilation is that of
local compatibility
, i.e.:

\mbox{Tr}_{I\setminus J}(\rho_{I})=\mbox{Tr}_{J\setminus I}(\rho_{J}),\mbox{ for all }I,J\in{\cal I}.

(14)

Given
{\cal A},{\cal I}
, we denote by
S_{\cal I}
the cone of (unnormalized) state ensembles
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
satisfying the compatibility conditions (
14
). We further denote by
S_{\cal I}^{1}
the convex set of normalized locally compatible state ensembles:
S_{\cal I}^{1}=\left\{\{\rho_{I}\}_{I}\in S_{\cal I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Tr}(\rho_{I})=1\;\forall I\in{\cal I}\right\}
.

We say that
\{\omega_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}^{\prime}\}\in S_{{\cal I}^{\prime}}
is an
ensemble dilation
of
\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}\}
if it satisfies local compatibility and, for each
I\in{\cal I}
there exists
I^{\prime}\in{\cal I}^{\prime}
such that
I^{\prime}\supseteq I
and
\mbox{Tr}_{I^{\prime}\setminus I}(\omega_{I^{\prime}})=\rho_{I}
. To express this index condition below, we write
{\cal I}^{\prime}\geq{\cal I}
. Also, to simplify our notation in the following pages, from now on we call
\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal I}}(\cdot)
any linear map that, applied to any dilation
\{\omega_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}^{\prime}\}
, returns the marginalized ensemble
\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}\}
. That is, for any
\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}\}\in S_{{\cal I}}
and any dilation
\{\omega_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}^{\prime}\}\in S_{{\cal I}^{\prime}}
thereof, it holds that
\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal I}}(\{\omega_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}^{\prime}\})=\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}\}
.

Let
H
be a linear function
H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{{\cal I}}\to\mathbb{R}
. The dual of the marginal problem is the
local Hamiltonian problem
:

\displaystyle\min_{\{\rho_{I}\}}H(\{\rho_{I}\}_{I}),

s.t.

\displaystyle\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}\}\in S_{\cal I}^{1},\mbox{ admits a state dilation}.

(15)

In order to solve
Section

III.1
our approach is to replace the state dilation constraint by a computationally tractable one. In order to do so we first rewrite it as a hierarchy of constraints between a sequence of dilations up to the global state itself
{\cal I}\leq{\cal J}_{1}\ldots\leq{\cal J}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={\cal A}
.
We then proceed analogously to what was already outlined in
Section

II
: applying coarse-graining to each constraint and compressing the state ensembles.
We shall now describe this procedure in the general setting. As in the overview in
Section

II
we break down the exposition into three parts: We start with a single coarse-graining and compression step; we then describe how this step can be iterated; finally we discuss the choice of coarse-graining maps.

III.2
One Coarse-Graining and Compression Step

Consider the following problem in
Eq.

16
where the state ensemble
\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}\}
is constrained to be compatible with a state ensemble
\Omega_{\cal J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\omega_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}J\in{\cal J}\}
with
{\cal J}\geq{\cal I}
.

\begin{split}E_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}},\Omega_{\cal J}}H_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}})\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}\setminus{\cal I}}(\Omega_{\cal J})\\
&\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}\in S^{1}_{\cal I}\\
&\Omega_{\cal J}\in S_{\cal J}\end{split}

(16)

Let
B
be a linear map from ensembles of states on
{\cal I}
to ones on
\hat{{\cal I}}\subset 2^{\hat{{\cal A}}}
, where

\hat{{\cal A}}
contains
{\cal A}
and additional auxiliary letters—the target sites of the coarse-graining. Let
B
act on ensembles element-wise as follows

B(\mathrm{P}_{\cal I})=\{B^{(I)}(\rho_{I})|I\in{\cal I}\}
,
where each
B^{(I)}(\rho_{I})
is supported on
\hat{I}\subset\hat{{\cal A}}
.
In order for
B(\mathrm{P}_{\cal I})
to be an ensemble of states (up to normalization) we require all
\{B^{(I)}\}
to be completely positive maps.
Note that applying
B
to both sides of the constraint
\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}\setminus{\cal I}}(\Omega_{\cal J})
appearing in
Eq.

16
relaxes it as
B
might be not injective.
Next, we require another coarse-graining map
C\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{\cal J}\rightarrow{\cal S}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}
. We say that
C
extends
B
to
\Omega_{{\cal J}}
if it satisfies:

B\circ\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}\setminus{\cal I}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\hat{{\cal J}}\setminus\hat{{\cal I}}}\circ C\;.

(17)

In that case we can rewrite the relaxed constraint

B(\mathrm{P}_{\cal I})=B(\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}\setminus{\cal I}}(\Omega_{\cal J}))

as

B(\mathrm{P}_{\cal I})=\mbox{Tr}_{\hat{{\cal J}}\setminus\hat{{\cal I}}}(C(\Omega_{\cal J}))
.
In the latter equation
\Omega_{{\cal J}}
appears only under the coarse-graining map which implies that some degrees of freedom of
\Omega_{{\cal J}}
are ignored in this constraint.
We can therefore impose a relaxation of this constraint using less memory by substituting
C(\Omega_{{\cal J}})
with

\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}\in S_{\hat{{\cal J}}}
.
As a result we have

\begin{split}E_{0}\geq\hat{E}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}},\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}}H_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}})\\
\text{s.t. }&B(\mathrm{P}_{\cal I})=\mbox{Tr}_{\hat{{\cal J}}\setminus\hat{{\cal I}}}(\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}})\\
&\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}\in S^{1}_{\cal I}\\
&\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}\in S_{\hat{{\cal J}}}\end{split}

(18)

What we have achieved in this single coarse-graining step is to relate our base ensemble
\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}}
(the one on which the Hamiltonian is evaluated) to an ensemble of smaller dimensions
\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}
than the original one
\Omega_{{\cal J}}
. Whether or not the new variable constrains
\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}}
in a useful way is determined by the choice of the coarse-graining map
B
. We shall return to this issue later after describing the full method.

For concreteness we now give two examples of coarse-graining maps which satisfy
the condition in
Eq.

17
.
These examples will serve us when we implement the full method in the 1D setting and each will produce a different variant of the general procedure. The first example was already introduced in
Section

II
(see
Eq.

7
). Here it is presented in full detail.

Example 1: CP coarse-graining maps

Let
H
be a nearest-neighbors Hamiltonian acting on a spin chain of length
4
:
H=h_{\{1,2\}}+h_{\{2,3\}}+h_{\{3,4\}}
and let
W^{\{o\}}_{\{i,j\}}
be a completely positive map acting on the two spins at sites
\{i,j\}
and mapping them to a new single site labeled
o
.
Consider the following marginal scenarios and coarse-graining maps:

\begin{split}{\cal A}&=\{1,2,3,4\}\\
\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}&=\{\rho_{\{1,2,3\}},\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\}\\
\Omega_{\cal J}&=\{\omega_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\}\end{split}\hskip 20.0pt\begin{split}B^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}},&I=\{1,2,3\}\\
W^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{4},&I=\{2,3,4\}\\
\end{cases}\\
C^{(J)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{4},&J=\{1,2,3,4\}\end{cases}\end{split}\hskip 20.0pt\begin{split}\hat{{\cal A}}&=\{1,2,3,4,o\}\\
\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}&=\{\hat{\omega}_{\{1,o,4\}}\}\end{split}

(19)

The Hamiltonian is evaluated on
\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}}
by tracing each term against the marginal of the appropriate state.
In this setting the relation
Eq.

17
is satisfied trivially because the partial trace and
B
always act on different sites, and
\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}\in S_{\hat{{\cal J}}}
trivially. The original problem and its relaxation in this setting are as follows

\begin{split}E_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}},\Omega_{{\cal J}}}H_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}})\\
\text{s.t. }&\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}\left(\omega_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\right)\\
&\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}\left(\omega_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\right)\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}\left(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}\left(\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\right)\\
&\rho_{\{1,2,3\}},\rho_{\{2,3,4\}},\omega_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\geq 0\\
&\mbox{Tr}\left(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}\left(\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\right)=1\end{split}\hskip 40.0pt\begin{split}\hat{E}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}},\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}}H_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}})\\
\text{s.t. }&{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}}\left(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}\left(\hat{\omega}_{\{1,o,4\}}\right)\\
&W^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{4}\left(\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}\left(\hat{\omega}_{\{1,o,4\}}\right)\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}\left(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}\left(\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\right)\\
&\rho_{\{1,2,3\}},\rho_{\{2,3,4\}},\hat{\omega}_{\{1,o,4\}}\geq 0\\
&\mbox{Tr}\left(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}\left(\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\right)=1\end{split}

(20)

Example 2: trace preserving coarse-graining maps

For the second example let
H
be nearest-neighbors Hamiltonian on a chain of length 8 and let
V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o\}}_{\{i,j\}}
be a completely positive and trace preserving map (CPTP) acting on the two spins at sites
\{i,j\}
and mapping them to a new single site labeled
o
. consider the following marginal scenarios and coarse-graining maps:

\begin{split}{\cal A}&=\{1,\ldots,8\}\\
\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}&=\{\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}},\rho_{\{3,4,5,6\}},\rho_{\{5,6,7,8\}}\}\\
\Omega_{\cal J}&=\{\omega_{\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}}\}\\
\hat{{\cal A}}&=\{1,\ldots,8,o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},o_{4}\}\\
\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}&=\{\hat{\omega}_{\{o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},o_{4}\}}\}\end{split}\hskip 20.0pt\begin{split}B^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}},&I=\{1,2,3,4\}\\
V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}},&I=\{3,4,5,6\}\\
V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}},&I=\{5,6,7,8\}\end{cases}\\
C^{(J)}&=\begin{cases}V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}},&J=\{1,\ldots,8\}\end{cases}\\
\end{split}

(21)

The map
C
maps the chain of 8 spins into a chain of length 4 by mapping systems
1,2
into
o_{1}
,
3,4
into
o_{2}
, and so on. In this setting the relation
Eq.

17
is satisfied because
V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}}
is trace preserving and therefore taking the partial trace before applying
V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}}
is the same as doing so after applying it. We will use equalities such as for example

\displaystyle V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})

\displaystyle=V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\left(\mbox{Tr}_{\{5,6,7,8\}}(\omega_{\{1,\ldots,8\}})\right)

(22)

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}_{\{o_{3},o_{4}\}}\left(V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}}(\omega_{\{1,\ldots,8\}})\right)

The original problem and its relaxation in this setting read as follows (for brevity we shall not write out the local compatibility constraint for
\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}}
explicitly).

\begin{split}E_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}},\Omega_{{\cal J}}}H_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}})\\
\text{s.t. }&\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{5,6,7,8\}}\left(\omega_{\{1,\ldots,8\}}\right)\\
&\rho_{\{3,4,5,6\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,2,7,8\}}\left(\omega_{\{1,\ldots,8\}}\right)\\
&\rho_{\{5,6,7,8\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\left(\omega_{\{1,\ldots,8\}}\right)\\
&\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}\in S^{1}_{\cal I},\;\omega_{\{1,\ldots,8\}}\geq 0\end{split}\hskip 40.0pt\begin{split}\hat{E}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}},\hat{\Omega}_{\hat{{\cal J}}}}H_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P}_{{\cal I}})\\
\text{s.t. }&V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\left(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{o_{3},o_{4}\}}\left(\hat{\omega}_{\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{4}\}}\right)\\
&V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\left(\rho_{\{3,4,5,6\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{o_{1},o_{4}\}}\left(\hat{\omega}_{\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{4}\}}\right)\\
&V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes V^{\text{\tiny{TP}}\{o_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}}\left(\rho_{\{5,6,7,8\}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{\{o_{1},o_{2}\}}\left(\hat{\omega}_{\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{4}\}}\right)\\
&\mathrm{P}_{\cal I}\in S^{1}_{\cal I},\;\hat{\omega}_{\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{4}\}}\geq 0\end{split}

(23)

III.3
Iterative Coarse-Graining and Compression

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{1}\equiv P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}(\Omega_{1})\overset{!}{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{2}\hookleftarrow C_{2}(P_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}(\Omega_{2})\overset{!}{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{3}\hookleftarrow C_{3}(P_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}(\Omega_{3})\overset{!}{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{4}\hookleftarrow C_{4}(P_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{1}\setminus{\cal I}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{2}\setminus{\cal I}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{3}\setminus{\cal I}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{4}\setminus{\cal I}_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{2}\setminus{\cal J}_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{3}\setminus{\cal J}_{2}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{4}\setminus{\cal J}_{3}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}

Figure 2:
The general relaxation scheme. The top row in the diagram shows the marginalization constraints (horizontal arrows) between the states ensembles
\mathrm{P}_{m}
appearing in the unrelaxed problem
Eq.

24
.
The coarse-graining maps
C_{m}
and
B_{m}
are indicated by the vertical arrows. The maps are chosen such that the diagram is commutative due to
Eq.

25
.
The coarse-grained constraints can be encoded using smaller dimensional variables
\Omega_{m}
that substitute
C_{m}(\mathrm{P}_{m})
. The relaxation is then defined by the constraints between
\Omega_{m}
along the diagonal which are indicated by
\overset{!}{=}
.

In the previous section we described how a coarse-graining map can be used to relax a constraint involving two state ensembles and to reduce the size of one of the variables.
Our aim is to apply coarse-graining in order to relax problem (
III.1
) and reduce the dimension of the exponentially large optimization variable that encodes the state dilation constraint
\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal A}\setminus I}\sigma=\rho_{I}
.
This, however, cannot be done in a single coarse-graining step.
Rather, we shall coarse-grain the global state iteratively employing a renormalization procedure.
The renormalization group structure—that a certain coarse-graining map is applied over and over again—will allow us to iterate the relaxation step described in
Section

III.2
above.

In the following we describe a relaxation procedure which is constructed using a renormalization scheme as input (i.e. an increasing sequence of coarse-graining maps constructed iteratively).
Given such a scheme we construct an increasing sequence of dilations
{\cal I}_{0}\leq{\cal I}_{1}\leq\ldots
which is compatible with the renormalization procedure in a sense that will be defined precisely below.
We first rewrite the constraint in the exact problem
Section

III.1
as a hierarchy of constraints between consecutive dilations.
We can then apply the single relaxation step to each pair of consecutive constraints in a consistent way by coarse-graining each increasing dilation further with the corresponding coarse-graining map.

We find it more instructive to first assume that the dilations and coarse-graining maps are compatible and describe the relaxation procedure. Then the conditions that need to be satisfied between the two arise naturally.
Let, therefore,
{\cal I}={\cal I}_{0}\leq{\cal I}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq{\cal I}_{n-1}\leq{\cal I}_{n}
be an increasing sequence of dilations and for
m=0,\ldots,n
denote by
\mathrm{P}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\rho_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in{\cal I}_{m}\}
the corresponding state ensembles. For a finite alphabet we set
{\cal I}_{n}=\{{\cal A}\}
and for an infinite one consider an infinite sequence of dilations and truncate it at a finite level
n
.
Our starting point for the relaxation procedure is the following

\begin{split}&\min_{\{\mathrm{P}_{m}\}_{m}}H(\mathrm{P}_{0}),\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mathrm{P}_{0}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{1}\setminus{\cal I}_{0}}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\\
&\mathrm{P}_{m}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal I}_{m}}(\mathrm{P}_{m+1}),\;\forall m\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\\
&\mathrm{P}_{0}\in{\cal S}_{{\cal I}_{0}}^{1},\\
&\mathrm{P}_{m}\in S_{{\cal I}_{m}}\;\forall m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\;.\end{split}

(24)

In the finite alphabet case this is just a reformulation of the exact problem
Section

III.1
with many extra variables.
For
|{\cal A}|=\infty

Eq.

24
is a relaxation of
Section

III.1
.
Rewriting the problem in this way allows us to apply the coarse-graining and compression steps described in
Section

III.2
above on every pair
\mathrm{P}_{m},\mathrm{P}_{m+1}
. As the dilations increase with
m
, we can apply larger coarse-graining maps between consecutive pairs.
We shall now describe the compatibility conditions that need to be satisfied between the dilations
\{{\cal I}_{m}\}
and a given coarse-graining scheme.

To every
{\cal I}_{m}
two coarse-graining maps are associated

\displaystyle C_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{{\cal I}_{m}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow S_{{\cal J}_{m}}

\displaystyle B_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{{\cal J}_{m}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow S_{{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m}}

where
{\cal J}_{m}
and
{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m}
are scenarios on alphabets that include additional sites that are the targets of coarse-graining maps.
Each map
B_{m}
is to be understood as implementing an additional coarse-graining to the output of
C_{m}
(an additional renormalization step); and
C_{m+1}
should be thought of as implementing the same level of coarse-graining as the composition
B_{m}\circ C_{m}
on the next ensemble (see
Fig.

2
).

Explicitly, we start with
C_{1}={\mathbb{I}}
and
{\cal J}_{1}={\cal I}_{1}
and require that
C_{2}
be an extension of
B_{1}
to
{\cal I}_{2}
i.e.
B_{1}
and
C_{2}
play the roles of
B
and
C
in
Eq.

18
and satisfy
Eq.

17
.
This pair of maps is used to relax and compress the constraint between
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
, replacing it by the constraint
B_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{1})=\Omega_{2}\in S_{{\cal J}_{2}}
, where
\Omega_{2}
is the compression of
C_{2}(\mathrm{P}_{2})
.
Next we require that
C_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{{\cal I}_{3}}\rightarrow S_{{\cal J}_{3}}
be an extension of the composition
B_{2}\circ C_{2}
to
{\cal I}_{3}
.
We can thus use
B_{2}\circ C_{2}
and
C_{3}
to relax the constraint between
\mathrm{P}_{2}
and
\mathrm{P}_{3}
and express it in terms of
\Omega_{2}
and
\Omega_{3}\in S_{{\cal J}_{3}}
.
We continue in this manner up to
m=n-1
. We require that for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

B_{m}\circ C_{m}\circ\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal I}_{m}}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m}}\circ C_{m+1}

(25)

Each constraint is relaxed in turn by the following steps

\begin{split}\mathrm{P}_{m}&=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal I}_{m}}(\mathrm{P}_{m+1})\\
&\Downarrow{\scriptstyle\text{apply }B_{m}\circ C_{m}}\\
B_{m}(C_{m}(\mathrm{P}_{m}))&=B_{m}(C_{m}(\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal I}_{m}}(\mathrm{P}_{m+1})))\\
&\Updownarrow{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:intertwineK}}\\
B_{m}(C_{m}(\mathrm{P}_{m}))&=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m}}(C_{m+1}(\mathrm{P}_{m+1}))\\
&\Downarrow{\scriptstyle C_{j}(S_{{\cal I}_{j}})\subset S_{{\cal J}_{j}}\forall j}\\
B_{m}(\Omega_{m})&=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m}}(\Omega_{m+1})\end{split}

(26)

The diagram in
Fig.

2
describes how the relaxed constraints between the state ensembles
\Omega_{m}
are obtained from the original ones between
\mathrm{P}_{m}
through the steps in
Eq.

26
.
We obtain the following relaxation of
Eq.

24
(and therefore also of
Section

III.1
).

\begin{split}&\min_{\mathrm{P}_{0},\{\Omega_{m}\}_{m}}H(\mathrm{P}_{0}),\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mathrm{P}_{0}\in S_{{\cal I}_{0}}^{1},\\
&\mathrm{P}_{0}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{1}\setminus{\cal I}_{0}}(\Omega_{1})\\
&B_{m}(\Omega_{m})=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{m+1}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m}}(\Omega_{m+1}),\;\forall m\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\\
&\Omega_{m}\in{S}_{{\cal J}_{m}},\;\forall m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\end{split}

(27)

III.4
Choosing the Coarse-Graining Maps

So far we explained how any set of maps
C_{m},B_{m}
that satisfy the conditions represented in
Fig.

2
provides a relaxation.
Clearly, for the relaxation in
Eq.

27
to be of any use we will have to choose the maps aptly. To see why certain choices of maps are ill-advised consider for example the case when
B_{1}
annihilates
\mathrm{P}_{1}^{*}
the state attaining the minimum energy
E_{1}
of the problem in
Eq.

24
with
n=1
.
Then the assignment
\mathrm{P}_{0}=\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal I}_{1}\setminus{\cal I}_{0}}\mathrm{P}_{1}^{*},\Omega_{1}=\mathrm{P}_{1}^{*}
and
\Omega_{m}=0
for
m>1
satisfies the constraints in
Eq.

27
and its minimum is therefore not greater than
E_{1}
. In this case we would not gain anything from optimizing over ensembles larger than
\mathrm{P}_{1}
because of the poor choice of
B_{1}
.

Later in
Section

V.2
we will show how, having solved
Eq.

27
once, one can then maximize the energy with respect to the maps
B_{m}
while holding the state ensembles fixed. One can then alternate between optimizing over states and maps, increasing the energy at every step. Our numerical experiments showed that while this does improve the energy, one cannot go far beyond the value obtained with the initially chosen maps. Therefore, one cannot start with random maps and find the optimal ones in this way.
A good initial guess for the coarse-graining maps is required. In order to find candidates for such maps we turn to other known numerical renormalization algorithms.

There is a close connection between numerical renormalization algorithms and certain families of tensor-network states
Cirac and Verstraete 2009
. Exemplary of this connection is the reformulation of the density matrix renormalization group algorithm (DMRG) in terms of a variational minimization over a family of matrix-product states (MPS)
Schollwöck 2011
. What is essential for our purpose is the idea that the tensors encoding an MPS can be regarded as a description of a renormalization procedure. It provides a numerical prescription for how to coarse-grain a spin chain while adding one extra spin at each step.
Similarly, other tensor-network states describe coarse-graining schemes when one views them as maps from regions of physical spins on a lattice to the virtual degrees of freedom on the bonds.

In
Section

IV
below we will show how to construct maps satisfying the conditions in
Fig.

2
from uniform matrix-product states
Zauner-Stauber et al. 2018
and tree-tensor-network states
Tagliacozzo et al. 2009
;
Milsted et al. 2019
.
The construction generalizes to other tensor networks also in higher spatial dimensions.
We will discuss the prospect of applying our method to 2D systems in
Section

VII
.

We thus see that tensor networks provide us with a variational family of coarse-graining maps to choose from. The question remains how to choose the best maps for a given Hamiltonian.
It seems at least plausible that an accurate tensor-network approximation of the ground-state should perform well as a coarse-graining scheme for our relaxation. In the case of MPS, contractions of consecutive tensors—when seen as a map from physical spins to virtual bonds—define subspaces in the Hilbert space of the physical spins where the approximate ground state is supported. If the true ground state is not far from the approximation, those subspaces should give a good approximation for its support and therefore should not contain states with locally high energy. While we cannot make this into a rigorous statement, this reasoning does seems to be confirmed by our numerical results.

We observed that while there is correlation between lower variational energy and tighter lower bounds from the resulting relaxation, there is no simple rule relating the two. For some models we clearly observed that the optimal variational state performed worse than others. Examples of this can be seen in
Fig.

6
below.
In the MPS-based implementation the difference was never significant and we therefore used the variationally optimal MPS to construct the coarse-graining maps.
In the case of the tree-tensor-network-based implementation the discrepancy between variational optimum and the tightness of the lower bound was more pronounced. In this case we found that a better lower bound was obtained by using tensors that have not converged fully to the optimal variational solution.

IV
Application to 1D translation-invariant models

We will now discuss how to apply the method described in
Section

III
to 1D translation-invariant systems.
We will present relaxation schemes based on two different renormalization procedures.
The first one is based on the DMRG algorithm and uses MPS—the corresponding variational ansatz—to construct the coarse-graining maps. We have already introduced it in
Section

II
, and re-state it here in the formalism of
Section

III
.
The second scheme is based on real-space renormalization and uses tree-tensor-network states as coarse-graining maps.

The discussion of two different renormalization schemes for the same problem does not only serve to demonstrate the versatility of the approach, but we will also see later that the different renormalization schemes behave differently with regard to, e.g., their generalization to higher dimensions, non-TI systems, or the possibility to optimize the renormalization maps.

IV.1
The translation-invariant local Hamiltonian problem

The TI problem was already introduced in
Section

II
. We briefly repeat it here for convenience.
The task is to minimize the energy density of a
k
-local Hamiltonian, with interaction term
h
, with respect to translation-invariant states:

\begin{split}E_{\text{TI}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min_{\rho}&\mbox{Tr}\left(h\rho\right)\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\rho\geq 0,\mbox{Tr}(\rho)=1\;,\\
&\rho\mbox{ admits a TI\ state dilation}\end{split}

(28)

This problem can be thought of as the limit of a sequence of finite size problems in which one requires that the state
\rho
is the marginal of an
n
-body state
\sigma
, and that the latter satisfies local translation invariance (LTI), which is just the translation-invariant version of the local compatibility condition
Eq.

14
for the ensemble of all consecutive
n
-body states of the chain. For any finite
n\geq k
the following is a relaxation of
Eq.

28
.

\begin{split}E_{\text{LTI}}(n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min_{\rho,\sigma_{\{1,2,\ldots,n\}}}&\mbox{Tr}\left(h\rho\right)\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mbox{Tr}(\rho)=1\;,\\
&\rho=\mbox{Tr}_{\{k+1,\ldots n\}}\sigma_{\{1,2,\ldots n\}}\\
&\sigma_{\{1,2,\ldots,n\}}\geq 0,\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\sigma_{\{1,2,\ldots,n\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{n\}}(\sigma_{\{1,2,\ldots,n\}}).\end{split}

(29)

In the following we describe both variants of the method in detail. The results obtained with the two methods are presented in
Section

VI
below.

IV.2
Variant 1: Coarse-Graining One Spin at a Time Using MPS

This is the variant that was already presented in
Section

II
. Here we provide more details and describe it in terms of the general framework presented in
Section

III
.
As in
Section

III
we start by rewriting the LTI problem as a hierarchy of constraints. Later we shall introduce the appropriate coarse-graining maps.
We expand
Eq.

29
as follows

\begin{split}E_{\text{LTI}}(n)=\min_{\{\rho\}}&\mbox{Tr}\left(h\rho_{\{1,\ldots,k\}}\right)\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mbox{Tr}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,k\}})=1\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots m\}})=\rho_{\{2,\ldots m\}}=\rho_{\{1,\ldots m-1\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{m\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots m\}}),\;\forall m\in\{k+1,\ldots,n\}\\
&\rho_{\{1,\ldots,m\}}\geq 0,\;\forall m\in\{k,\ldots,n\}\;.\end{split}

(30)

where we encoded the LTI property of the largest state by requiring that at every step taking the left marginal results in the same state as taking the right one.
The constraints in
Eq.

30
and
Eq.

29
are exactly equivalent because an
n
-body state satisfies the LTI constraint iff all its
m
-body marginals for
m\leq n
satisfy it as well.

In order to satisfy the construction in
Fig.

2
we need a collection of maps that act on increasing strings of spins
\{1,\ldots,m\}
to play the role of the
C_{m}
maps. In addition we need maps that can be composed with
C_{m}
to coarse-grain one more spin—those will comprise the
B_{m}
maps. We construct such maps from the variational ground-state approximation output by the VUMPS algorithm
Zauner-Stauber et al. 2018
when applied to the same Hamiltonian. The algorithm produces a single rank-
3
tensor,
A
, with dimensions
(D,d,D)
which describes a translation-invariant global pure state of the infinite spin chain, where
D
is the bond dimension of the MPS (specified as an input to the algorithm) and
d
is the dimension of the physical spins.
First define the map
w^{\{o\}}_{\{1,2,\ldots,m\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\cal H}_{\{1,2,\ldots,m\}}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{o}
, where
{\cal H}_{o}={\cal H}_{o^{l}}\otimes{\cal H}_{o^{r}}
and both
{\cal H}_{o^{l}},{\cal H}_{o^{r}}
are of dimension
D
, by the contraction of
m
copies of
A
as follows

w^{\{o^{l},o^{r}\}}_{\{1,2,\ldots,m\}}\;\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\;\hbox to154.96pt{\vbox to34.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.51973pt\lower-22.55939pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(32)

Define the CP maps

W^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}},L^{\{y\}}_{\{1,o\}}
and
R^{\{x\}}_{\{o,m+1\}}
by the Kraus operators
w^{\{o\}}_{\{2,3\}},l^{\{y\}}_{\{1,o\}}
and
r^{\{x\}}_{\{o,m+1\}}
respectively (e.g.
W(X)=wX{w^{\dagger}}
).
Because contracting
m+1
copies of
A
can be done in either order we have

L^{\{o\}}_{\{1,o^{\prime}\}}\circ\left({\mathbb{I}}_{\{1\}}\otimes W^{\{o^{\prime}\}}_{\{2,\ldots,m+1\}}\right)=W^{\{o\}}_{\{1,\ldots,m+1\}}=R^{\{o\}}_{\{o^{\prime},m+1\}}\circ\left(W^{\{o^{\prime}\}}_{\{1,\ldots,m\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{\{m+1\}}\right)\;.

(33)

We can now define the maps
B_{m}
and
C_{m}
for
m=1,\ldots,n-3
. For concreteness we take
k=3
. (The marginal scenarios
{\cal J}
and
{\cal J}^{\prime}
are implicit in the subscripts of the
\omega
variables.)

\begin{split}\Omega_{1}&=\mathrm{P}_{1}=\{\rho_{\{1,2,3\}},\rho_{\{2,3,4\}}\}\\
\Omega_{2}&=\{\omega_{\{1,o_{4},4\}},\omega_{\{2,o_{4},5\}}\}\\
\Omega_{3}&=\{\omega_{\{1,o_{5},5\}},\omega_{\{2,o_{5},6\}}\}\\[5.0pt]
\Omega_{m}&=\{\omega_{\{1,o_{m+2},m+2\}},\omega_{\{2,o_{m+2},m+3\}}\}\\
\end{split}\hskip 20.0pt\begin{split}B_{1}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o_{4}\}}_{\{2,3\}},&I=\{1,2,3\}\\
W^{\{o_{4}\}}_{\{2,3\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{4},&I=\{2,3,4\}\end{cases}\\
C_{2}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o_{4}\}}_{\{2,3\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{4},&I=\{1,2,3,4\}\\
{\mathbb{I}}_{2}\otimes W^{\{o_{4}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{5},&I=\{2,3,4,5\}\end{cases}\\
B_{2}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes R^{\{o_{5}\}}_{\{o_{4},4\}},&I=\{1,o_{4},4\}\\
L^{\{o_{5}\}}_{\{2,o_{4}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{5},&I=\{2,o_{4},5\}\end{cases}\\[5.0pt]
C_{m}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o_{m+2}\}}_{\{2,\ldots,m+1\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{m+2},&I=\{1,\ldots,m+2\}\\
{\mathbb{I}}_{2}\otimes W^{\{o_{m+2}\}}_{\{3,\ldots,m+2\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{m+3},&I=\{2,\ldots,m+3\}\end{cases}\\
B_{m}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes R^{\{o_{m+3}\}}_{\{o_{m+2},m+2\}},&I=\{1,o_{m+2},m+2\}\\
L^{\{o_{m+3}\}}_{\{2,o_{m+2}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{m+3},&I=\{2,o_{m+2},m+3\}\end{cases}\end{split}

(34)

These satisfy
Fig.

2
due to
Eq.

33
and the conditions
\rho_{\{1,\ldots,m\}}=\rho_{\{2,\ldots,m+1\}}
.

Due to the LTI condition we have that
\omega_{1,o_{m+2},m+2}=\omega_{2,o_{m+2},m+3}
, so we just need to keep one variable (say, the former) and we can finally write the relaxation in terms of one variable for a state of each size:

\begin{split}\hat{E}_{\text{MPS}}^{(D)}(n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min&\mbox{Tr}(h\rho_{\{1,2,3\}})\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\rho_{\{1,2,3\}}\geq 0,\mbox{Tr}(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}})=1\;,\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{3\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}})\\
&{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes W^{\{o_{4}\}}_{\{2,3\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}(\omega_{\{1,o_{4},4\}})\\
&W^{\{o_{4}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{3}(\rho_{\{1,2,3\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\omega_{\{1,o_{4},4\}})\\
&\forall m\in\{5,\ldots n\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\\
&\left\{\begin{split}&{\mathbb{I}}_{1}\otimes R^{\{o_{m}\}}_{\{o_{m-1},m-1\}}(\omega_{\{1,o_{m-1},m-1\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{m\}}(\omega_{\{1,o_{m},m\}})\\
&L^{\{o_{m}\}}_{\{1,o_{m-1}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{m-1}(\omega_{\{1,o_{m-1},m-1\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\omega_{\{1,o_{m},m\}})\end{split}\right\}\\
&\omega_{\{1,o_{m},m\}}\geq 0,\;\forall m\in\{4,\ldots n\}\;.\end{split}

(35)

All of the matrices
\omega_{\{1,o_{m},m\}}
have dimensions
d^{2}D^{2}\times d^{2}D^{2}
, where
D
is the bond dimension of the MPS used to construct the coarse-graining maps. The number of variables in the problem is thus
O(nd^{4}D^{4})
.

Equation

13
shows the constraints appearing in
Eq.

35
(up to
m=5
) in diagrammatic notation.

Note that in
Eq.

35
the first coarse-graining map acts on two spins and maps them to a
D\times D
dimensional system. This only pays off (i.e. causes a reduction in size when substituting
\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}
by
\omega_{\{1,o_{4},4\}}
) if
D<d
. In practice this will not be the case as we apply the method mainly on spin
1/2
systems. In that case we simply start the hierarchy in
Eq.

30
from
\rho_{\{1,\ldots,k_{0}\}}
with the smallest
k_{0}
such that
d^{k_{0}-1}>D^{2}
.

IV.3
Variant 2: Real-Space Renormalization, Coarse-Graining Two Spins Into One

Starting from the LTI problem,
Eq.

29
, we apply a real-space renormalization procedure where at each step the system size is halved. For this we take
n=2^{N}
and first relax the LTI problem of size
n
as follows. For concreteness assume that the Hamiltonian is
k
-local with
k\leq 4
so that we can start from a
4
-body state at the base of the hierarchy. The energy can be evaluated from this state by averaging the sum of the translated terms
h
, e.g. for
k=3
define
H_{4}=({\mathbb{I}}\otimes h+h\otimes{\mathbb{I}})/2
.

\begin{split}\min_{\{\rho\}}&\mbox{Tr}\left(H_{4}\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\right)\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mbox{Tr}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})=1\;,\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})\\[5.0pt]
&\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{5,6,7,8\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,8\}})\\
&\rho_{\{3,4,5,6\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,2,7,8\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,8\}})\\
&\rho_{\{5,6,7,8\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,2,3,4\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,8\}})\\[5.0pt]
&\rho_{\{1,\ldots,8\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{9,\ldots,16\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,16\}})\\
&\rho_{\{5,\ldots,12\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,\ldots,4,13,\ldots,16\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,16\}})\\
&\rho_{\{9,\ldots,16\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,\ldots,8\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,16\}})\\
\vdots\\
&\rho_{\{1,\ldots,2^{N-1}\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{2^{N-1}+1,\ldots,2^{N}\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,2^{N}\}})\\
&\rho_{\{2^{N-2}+1,\ldots,3\times 2^{N-2}\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,\ldots,2^{N-2},3\times 2^{N-2}+1,\ldots,2^{N}\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,2^{N}\}})\\
&\rho_{\{2^{N-1}+1,\ldots,2^{N}\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,\ldots,2^{N-1}\}}(\rho_{\{1,\ldots,2^{N}\}})\\[5.0pt]
&\rho_{\{1,\ldots,2^{m-1}\}}=\rho_{\{2^{m-2}+1,\ldots,3\times 2^{m-2}\}}=\rho_{\{2^{m-1}+1,\ldots,2^{m}\}}\geq 0,\;\forall m\in\{2,\ldots N\}.\;\end{split}

(36)

In this formulation the
4
-body LTI states are constrained to be marginals of
8
-body states that are
2
-LTI i.e. locally translation invariant with respect to translation by
2
sites; the
8
-body states are in turn marginals of
16
-body states that are
4
-LTI and so on.

In order to coarse-grain every pair of spins in
\rho_{\{1,\ldots,2^{m}\}}
we will need to “coarse-grain under the partial trace” as was demonstrated in
Section

III.2
.
For this the coarse-graining maps need to be trace preserving (CPTP).
To construct the coarse-graining maps we use the output of a tree-tensor-network variational algorithm
Milsted et al. 2019
which optimizes the three-legged tensors making up the network layer by layer.
The entire tensor network describes a pure state on a ring of size
2^{L}
, where
L
is the number of layers, and within each layer the tensors are uniform and are partial isometries from two sites at the lower layer to one site at the higher layer. The output of the algorithm consists of
L
partial isometries
T_{l}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\cal H}_{l-1}\otimes{\cal H}_{l-1}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{l}

where
\dim{\cal H}_{0}=d
is the dimension of the physical spins and for all
L>l\geq 1

\dim{\cal H}_{l}\leq D
, where
D
is an input to the algorithm. At the last layer
\dim{\cal H}_{L}=1
such that
T_{L}
describes a pure two-body state on
{\cal H}_{L-1}\otimes{\cal H}_{L-1}
.

To make CPTP maps from the partial isometries
T_{l}
we start with
T_{1}
and increase the output Hilbert space dimension by one

{\cal H}_{2}\mapsto{\cal H}_{2}\oplus{\cal H}_{g}
where
{\cal H}_{g}
is spanned by one vector
|g\rangle
. For every vector
|k\rangle\in\ker(T_{1})
we define the Kraus operator
K_{k}=|g\rangle\langle k|
.
The following map is then CPTP

{V_{1}}(X)=T_{1}XT_{1}^{\dagger}+\sum_{k=1}^{\dim\ker(T_{1})}K_{k}XK_{k}^{\dagger}\;.

At the next layer we need to construct a map
{V_{2}}
acting on states on

({\cal H}_{2}\oplus{\cal H}_{g})\otimes({\cal H}_{2}\oplus{\cal H}_{g})
. We use
T_{2}
to define the first Kraus operator of
{V_{2}}
acting on the
{\cal H}_{2}\otimes{\cal H}_{2}
subspace and mapping it into the
{\cal H}_{3}
subspace in
{\cal H}_{3}\oplus{\cal H}_{g^{\prime}}
.
Then for every
|k\rangle
in

\ker(T_{2})\oplus({\cal H}_{2}\otimes{\cal H}_{g})\oplus({\cal H}_{g}\otimes{\cal H}_{2})\oplus({\cal H}_{g}\otimes{\cal H}_{g})

we construct a Kraus operator
|g^{\prime}\rangle\langle k|
mapping
|k\rangle
to
|g^{\prime}\rangle
in
{\cal H}_{3}\oplus{\cal H}_{g^{\prime}}
.
We iterate the last step using
T_{l}
to make
{V_{l}}
for
l=3,\ldots,L
.

We denote the target sites by
o^{m}_{1},\ldots,o^{m}_{8}
where
m
is the level of coarse-graining.
Further denote by
{\mathbb{V}_{m}}
the composition of
m
layers of the maps
{V_{l}}
as follows:

\begin{split}{\mathbb{V}_{1}}&\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={V_{1}}\\
{\mathbb{V}_{j}}&\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={V_{j}}\circ({\mathbb{V}_{j-1}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{j-1}})\ ,\text{for }j\in\{2,\ldots,N-2\}\ .\end{split}

(37)

The maps
{\mathbb{V}_{m}}
implement a coarse-graining of
2^{m}
spins mapping them to a site
o^{m}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,8\}
.
The maps
B_{m},C_{m}
are then defined as follows.
Figure

3
shows the first two maps.

\begin{split}\mathrm{P}_{1}&=\{\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}},\rho_{\{3,4,5,6\}},\rho_{\{5,6,7,8\}}\}\\
\Omega_{2}&=\{\omega_{\{o^{2}_{1},o^{2}_{2},o^{2}_{3},o^{2}_{4}\}}\}\\[5.0pt]
\Omega_{m}&=\{\omega_{\{o^{m}_{1},o^{m}_{2},o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}}\}\end{split}\hskip 20.0pt\begin{split}B_{1}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}},&I=\{1,2,3,4\}\\[4.0pt]
{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}},&I=\{3,4,5,6\}\\[4.0pt]
{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}},&I=\{5,6,7,8\}\end{cases}\\[4.0pt]
C_{2}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}},&I=\{1,\ldots,8\}\\
{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{3}\}}_{\{5,6\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{4}\}}_{\{7,8\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{5}\}}_{\{9,10\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{6}\}}_{\{11,12\}},&I=\{5,\ldots,12\}\\
{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{5}\}}_{\{9,10\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{6}\}}_{\{11,12\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{7}\}}_{\{13,14\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{8}\}}_{\{15,16\}},&I=\{9,\ldots,16\}\end{cases}\end{split}

(38)

\begin{split}\forall m&\in\{2,\ldots,N-2\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\\
B_{m}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{V_{m}}^{\{o^{m+1}_{1}\}}_{\{o^{m}_{1},o^{m}_{2}\}}\otimes{V_{m}}^{\{o^{m+1}_{2}\}}_{\{o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}},&I=\{o^{m}_{1},o^{m}_{2},o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}\\[4.0pt]
{V_{m}}^{\{o^{m+1}_{2}\}}_{\{o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}}\otimes{V_{m}}^{\{o^{m+1}_{3}\}}_{\{o^{m}_{5},o^{m}_{6}\}},&I=\{o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4},o^{m}_{5},o^{m}_{6}\}\\[4.0pt]
{V_{m}}^{\{o^{m+1}_{3}\}}_{\{o^{m}_{5},o^{m}_{6}\}}\otimes{V_{m}}^{\{o^{m+1}_{4}\}}_{\{o^{m}_{7},o^{m}_{8}\}},&I=\{o^{m}_{5},o^{m}_{6},o^{m}_{7},o^{m}_{8}\}\end{cases}\\[4.0pt]
\forall m&\in\{3,\ldots,N-1\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\\
C_{m}^{(I)}&=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{1}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{2}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{3}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{4}\}},&I=\{1,\ldots,2^{m+1}\}\\
{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{3}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{4}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{5}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{6}\}},&I=\{2^{m}+1,\ldots,3\times 2^{m}\}\\
{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{5}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{6}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{7}\}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\{o^{m}_{8}\}},&I=\{2^{m+1}+1,\ldots,2^{m+2}\}\end{cases}\;.\\[4.0pt]
\end{split}

, for

.

).

, and rewrite the problem in terms of one LTI state per level as

\begin{split}\hat{E}_{\text{TTN}}^{(D)}(n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min_{\rho,\{\omega\}}&\mbox{Tr}\left(H_{4}\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}\right)\\
\mbox{s.t. }&\mbox{Tr}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})=1\;,\\
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{4\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{1\}}(\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}})\\
&{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{1}\}}_{\{1,2\}}\otimes{V_{1}}^{\{o^{2}_{2}\}}_{\{3,4\}}\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}=\mbox{Tr}_{\{o^{2}_{1},o^{2}_{2}\}}(\omega_{\{o^{2}_{1},o^{2}_{2},o^{2}_{3},o^{2}_{4}\}})\\[4.0pt]
&\mbox{Tr}_{\{o^{m}_{4}\}}(\omega_{\{o^{m}_{1},o^{m}_{2},o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}})=\mbox{Tr}_{\{o^{m}_{1}\}}(\omega_{\{o^{m}_{1},o^{m}_{2},o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}}),\;\forall m\in\{2,\ldots,N-1\}\\[4.0pt]
