(39)

Figure

3
shows how
Eq.

39
is obtained from
Eq.

36
.
The matrices
\omega_{\{o^{m}_{1},o^{m}_{2},o^{m}_{3},o^{m}_{4}\}}
are of size at most
(D+1)^{4}\times(D+1)^{4}

which implies an
O(\log(n)D^{8})
scaling of the problem.

V
Optimizing the coarse-graining maps and certifying numerical solutions

In this section, we discuss two further numerical aspects of the optimization algorithms. First, we show that it is possible to include an optimization over the coarse-graining maps into the algorithm. This optimization takes itself the form of an SDP; thus, the entire optimization can be carried out as a sequence of alternating SDPs. Second, we discuss how the use of the dual SDP allows us to certify numerical solutions, yielding fully rigorous lower bounds.

V.1
Dual SDP

Both methods rely on the dual SDP of the relaxation in
Eq.

27
, which we shall now introduce.

Recall that in
Eq.

27
the variables are restricted to take values in the cones of locally compatible state ensembles:
{\Omega}_{m}\in{\cal S}_{{\cal J}_{m}}
(with an additional normalization constraint for
\mathrm{P}_{0}\in{\cal S}^{1}_{{\cal I}_{0}}
). In the dual SDP we will need to deal with their dual cones
{\cal S}^{*}_{\cal J}
.
For this purpose we represent
H,\mathrm{P}_{0},{\Omega}_{m}
as block-diagonal matrices. Let
\mathbb{M}^{+}_{\cal I}
denote the cone of complex positive semidefinite matrices on

{\cal I}
:
\mathbb{M}^{+}_{\cal I}=\{\bigoplus_{I\in{\cal I}}M_{I}|M_{I}\geq 0\}
.
Then the cone of locally compatible state ensembles on
{\cal I}
can be represented as

{\cal S}_{{\cal I}}=\left\{\mathrm{P}\in\mathbb{M}^{+}_{\cal I}\left|\mathbb{L}_{\cal I}(\mathrm{P})=0\right.\right\}

where
\mathbb{L}_{\cal I}
is a linear map encoding the local compatibility constraints specified in
Eq.

14
.
Its dual cone is then

{\cal S}_{{\cal I}}^{*}=\{\mathbb{M}_{\cal I}^{+}+\mathbb{L}^{*}_{\cal I}(X)|X\in\mathbb{M}_{\cal I}\}

where
(\cdot)^{*}
denotes the adjoint of a linear map and
\mathbb{M}_{\cal I}
is the space of block-diagonal matrices on
{\cal I}
.
A constraint of the form
Y\in{\cal S}_{{\cal I}}^{*}
can than be rewritten as

\exists X\in\mathbb{M}_{\cal I}\mbox{ s.t. }Y-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{\cal I}(X)\in\mathbb{M}_{\cal I}^{+}

by introducing an auxiliary variable
X
.
We denote by
\lambda_{m}
the Lagrange multiplier of the
m
th constraint in
Eq.

27
.
We write the normalization constraint explicitly as

\mbox{Tr}(\mathrm{P}_{0})=|{\cal I}_{0}|
,
denoting its Lagrange multiplier by
{\varepsilon}
. With this notation the dual of
Eq.

27
is

\begin{split}\max_{{\varepsilon},\{\lambda_{m}\},\{X_{m}\}}&({\varepsilon})\\
\mbox{s.t. }&H+\lambda_{0}-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{|{\cal I}_{0}|}-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{0}(X_{0})\in\mathbb{M}_{{\cal I}_{0}}^{+}\\
&B_{1}^{*}(\lambda_{1})-\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{1}\setminus{\cal I}_{0}}^{*}(\lambda_{0})-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{1}(X_{1})\in\mathbb{M}_{{\cal J}_{1}}^{+}\\
&B_{m}^{*}(\lambda_{m})-\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{m}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{m-1}}^{*}(\lambda_{m-1})-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{m}(X_{m})\in\mathbb{M}_{{\cal J}_{m}}^{+}\;,\forall m\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}\\
&-\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{n}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{n-1}}^{*}(\lambda_{n-1})-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{n}(X_{n})\in\mathbb{M}_{{\cal J}_{n}}^{+}\ .\end{split}

(40)

The solution of
Eq.

40
is always a lower bound to the solution of
Eq.

27
and the two are equal when strong duality holds
Vandenberghe and Boyd 1996
. In our setting the latter is the case as the constraints in
Eq.

27
are satisfied by choosing all the states to be proportional to the identity operator (which is positive definite).

V.2
Optimizing Over the Coarse-Graining Maps

As described in
Section

III.4
we have found an ansatz for the coarse-graining maps in the form of tensor networks, and we have provided heuristic arguments for choosing a tensor-network approximation to the ground state when formulating our relaxation.
Our numerical results show that while there is merit to this heuristic, the results can be improved by optimizing the coarse-graining maps. In the MPS-based method this was not necessary as the results obtained using the MPS ground-state approximation were very good. In the tree-tensor-network-based variant less so. We therefore implemented the following procedure to optimize the coarse-graining maps used in the tree-tensor-network-based variant.

The form of the dual problem
Eq.

40
suggests a simple scheme to optimize over the maps
\{B_{m}\}
. Namely, first solve the problem
Eq.

40
with fixed CP maps, hence obtaining the solution
{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\{\lambda^{(1)}_{m}\}
. Next, choose
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, fix the variable
\lambda_{j}=\lambda^{(1)}_{j}
and solve
Eq.

40
optimizing over
{\varepsilon},B_{j},\{X_{m}\}
and
\{\lambda_{m}\}_{m\neq j}
with additional positive semidefinite (and linear) constraints on the Choi representation of
B_{j}
to ensure it is CP (and TP).
This provides a solution
{\varepsilon}^{(2)},\lambda^{(2)}_{m},B^{(2)}_{j}
with objective value at least as high as the previous one, i.e.,
{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\geq{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
. Next we can either fix
B_{j}=B^{(2)}_{j}
and optimize again over all variables
\{\lambda_{m}\}
and
{\varepsilon}
or choose another index
j^{\prime}
and optimize
B_{j^{\prime}}
fixing
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}
. By conducting coordinate descent in this fashion, it is possible to improve the lower bound obtained with the initial coarse-graining maps.

We implemented this procedure in the tree-tensor-network-based example. In this setting there is an additional snag as the maps
B_{m}
are always of the form
{V}_{m}\otimes{V}_{m}
. This presents a problem since
{V}_{m}\otimes{V}_{m}
is not linear in
{V}_{m}
. To overcome this we parametrize
B_{m}
by two maps
V_{m}^{\prime}
and
V_{m}^{\prime\prime}
as follows

\tilde{B}_{m}(V_{m}^{\prime},V_{m}^{\prime\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\left(V_{m}^{\prime}\otimes V_{m}^{\prime\prime}+V_{m}^{\prime\prime}\otimes V_{m}^{\prime}\right)\;.

(41)

This parametrization is linear in both
V_{m}^{\prime}
and
V_{m}^{\prime\prime}
. We can then optimize over one map while holding the other one fixed and alternate between the two.
Allowing this more general form of the maps
\{B_{m}\}
instead of the one appearing in the relaxation
Eq.

39
(
B_{m}={V}_{m}\otimes{V}_{m}
) still results in a relaxation of
Eq.

36
.
To see this, note that each of the maps comprising the coarse-graining maps
C_{m}
, as defined in
Eqs.

37
and
38
, can be written as

{\mathbb{V}_{m-1}}^{\otimes 4}={V_{m-1}}^{\otimes 4}\circ{V_{m-2}}^{\otimes 8}\circ\ldots\circ{V_{1}}^{\otimes 2^{m}}
.
We can modify each layer
{V_{j}}^{\otimes 2l}
appearing in this expression and parametrize it in terms of two maps
V_{j}^{\prime}
and
V_{j}^{\prime\prime}
as follows

\frac{1}{2}{(V_{j}^{\prime}\otimes V_{j}^{\prime\prime})}^{\otimes l}+\frac{1}{2}{(V_{j}^{\prime\prime}\otimes V_{j}^{\prime})}^{\otimes l}
.
Each such layer preserves the LTI symmetry corresponding to its length scale when applied to the constraints in
Eq.

36
. (Translation by 2 sites, 4 sites and so on.)
The resulting maps, together with the maps defined in
Eq.

41
satisfy the required relations in
Fig.

2
.

V.3
Rigorous Bounds From Finite Solver Precision

In theory a solution to the relaxation in
Eq.

27
provides a rigorous lower bound to the problem in
Section

III.1
. However, when performing the numerical optimization the SDP solver will always be accurate up to some finite precision. Furthermore, for large-scale problems some SDP solvers exhibit slow convergence and one might wish to obtain a rigorous result from the last computed iteration and not wait for full convergence. According to basic SDP theory any feasible point of the dual of an SDP gives a lower bound to the primal’s optimal value
Vandenberghe and Boyd 1996
.
To obtain a rigorous bound from a numerically obtained solution it is sufficient to take the dual variable produced by the solver and modify it to make it strictly feasible.

In our scheme this can be done by using the structure of the dual problem
Eq.

40
. Starting from a candidate solution
({\varepsilon},\{\lambda_{m}\},\{X_{m}\})
produced by the solver, which is not known to be feasible, we can check if it satisfies the constraints in
Eq.

40
by exact diagonalization of the matrices involved (each of the blocks is typically of modest size). If it is not feasible, we can correct it as follows: Starting from the last constraint, we replace
\lambda_{n-1}\mapsto\tilde{\lambda}_{n-1}=\lambda_{n-1}-e_{n-1}{\mathbb{I}}
, where
e_{n-1}
is the minimal eigenvalue of

-\mbox{Tr}_{{\cal J}_{n}\setminus{\cal J}^{\prime}_{n-1}}^{*}(\lambda_{n-1})-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{n}(X_{n})
. After this substitution the last constraint is satisfied because
\mbox{Tr}_{\tilde{{\cal I}}_{n}\setminus{\cal I}_{n-1}}^{*}
just inserts tensor products with identity matrices in the appropriate blocks of
\lambda_{n-1}
.
We then proceed to compute
e_{n-2}
, the minimum eigenvalue of the second to last constraint after the substitution with
\tilde{\lambda}_{n-1}
, and replace
\lambda_{n-2}\mapsto\tilde{\lambda}_{n-2}=\lambda_{n-2}-e_{n-2}{\mathbb{I}}
.
We proceed in this way until in the last step we add the minimum eigenvalue of
H+\lambda_{0}-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{|{\cal I}_{0}|}-\mathbb{L}^{*}_{0}(X_{0})
to
{\varepsilon}
, the objective energy function, thereby satisfying all constraints with a modified set of variables and a possibly lower bound on the ground state energy.

Note that after the substitution
\lambda_{m}\mapsto\tilde{\lambda}_{m}=\lambda_{m}-e_{m}{\mathbb{I}}
, a term
e_{m}B_{m}^{*}({\mathbb{I}})
is added to the previous constraint which can inflate the next required correction
e_{m-1}
.
However, if the maps
B_{m}
satisfy
B^{*}_{m}({\mathbb{I}})\leq{\mathbb{I}}
, i.e. if
B_{m}
are trace non-increasing, then the errors of all the individual constraints simply add up. In our MPS-based implementation this condition on
B_{m}
can be satisfied by transforming the MPS into e.g. the left gauge (see Ref.
Zauner-Stauber et al. 2018
for details) before constructing the coarse-graining maps. In the tree-tensor-network-based implementation this condition is satisfied as the maps used there are trace preserving. In theory, gauge transformations of the MPS should not change the value of the relaxation
Eq.

35
as the constraints before and after changing the gauge are related by an invertible map. In practice, however, the numerical result can be affected by the choice of gauge if the map implementing the gauge transformation is poorly conditioned.

Finally note that depending on the specific scenario there might be other ways to modify the dual solution to make it strictly feasible. In our examples shifting the
\{\lambda_{m}\}
variables by a constant is the straightforward choice since the
\{X_{m}\}
variables correspond to the LTI constraint, meaning they appear in the equations as
\mathbb{L}_{m}^{*}(X_{m})={\mathbb{I}}\otimes X_{m}-X_{m}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}
and shifting them by a constant does not affect the constraints.

VI
Benchmarking the Method on 1D Hamiltonians

In this section, we present numerical results obtained with our relaxation method on a broad range of models, where we have implemented the two variants of the method that were described in
Section

IV
(MPS and TTN). In addition to the results themselves, we also provide a detailed analysis of the performance of the method.

VI.1
Studied models

We have studied the following 1D spin models: The critical Ising model, the Heisenberg antiferromagnet, the XX model, the XXZ model in the symmetry broken Ising phase (the last three all being instances of the XXZ chain), a critical spin-
1/2

J_{1}
-
J_{2}
model, and the spin-
1
Haldane chain.
The precise Hamiltonians used are

\displaystyle H_{\text{TFI}}(h_{z})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}S^{X}_{i}S^{X}_{i+1}-\frac{h_{Z}}{2}\sum_{i}S^{Z}_{i}

Transverse field Ising model (TFI)

\displaystyle H_{\text{XXZ}}^{S}(\Delta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}S^{X}_{i}S^{X}_{i+1}+S^{Y}_{i}S^{Y}_{i+1}+\Delta S^{Z}_{i}S^{Z}_{i+1}

\displaystyle\text{XXZ spin }S\text{ chain}

(42)

\displaystyle H_{\text{J${}_{1}$-J${}_{2}$}}(J_{1},J_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}J_{1}\vec{S}_{i}\cdot\vec{S}_{i+1}+J_{2}\vec{S}_{i}\cdot\vec{S}_{i+2}

\displaystyle\text{J}_{1}\text{-J}_{2}\text{ Heisenberg spin }1/2\text{ chain}

(with
\vec{S}_{i}\cdot\vec{S}_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{X}_{i}S^{X}_{j}+S^{Y}_{i}S^{Y}_{j}+S^{Z}_{i}S^{Z}_{j}
, and
S^{X}_{i},S^{Y}_{i},S^{Z}_{i}
the spin operators acting on site
i
of the chain, with eigenvalues
\pm 1/2
for spin
1/2
).
An overview of of all the models and the corresponding parameters is given in

VI.2
Numerical Results

We solved the MPS-based relaxation,
Eq.

35
, for all of the above models, and the tree-tensor-network-based relaxation,
Eq.

39
, for models
\mathbf{a\text{ through }d}
.
For each model, we solved the relaxations for various values of
n
and bond dimensions
D
; we denote the obtained
energy densities by
{E}^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{MPS/TTN}}(n,D)
.
For comparison, we have also computed the LTI energy density
E_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}
of the exact SDP hierarchy truncated at the
n
-th level,
Eq.

5
. Since
both variants of our method are relaxations of the LTI problem, it holds that

{E}^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{MPS/TTN}}(n,D)\leq E_{\text{LTI}}{(n)}
.

For both methods, we have used a variationally optimized tensor network to construct the coarse-graining maps (obtained by VUMPS and the TTN algorithm in Refs.
Tagliacozzo et al. 2009
;
Milsted et al. 2019
, respectively); In the case of TTN, we have additionally implemented
the optimization over the coarse-graining maps described in
Section

V.2
.
For certain systems, the optimization with VUMPS can fail to converge for specific bond dimensions
D
for a single-site unit cell (see
Zauner-Stauber et al. 2018
). In some cases, this could be remedied by a sublattice rotation [specifically, a
\pi
rotation around the
z
-axis on every second spin which transforms
H^{1/2}_{\text{XXZ}}(\Delta)
to
-H^{1/2}_{\text{XXZ}}(-\Delta)
]. Yet, VUMPS would not converge for all values of
D
, leading to missing data points (
D=5,7
in model
c
and
D=2,3
in models
d
and
f
).

Fig.

4
shows the distance of the obtained lower bounds from the actual ground state energy density
E_{\text{TI}}
, denoted by

\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{MPS/TTN}}(n,D)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{\text{TI}}-E^{\text{relax}}_{\text{MPS/TTN}}(D,n)
and

\Delta E_{\text{LTI}}=E_{\text{TI}}-E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
, respectively, for all six models. Where known, we used the
exact ground state energy density for
E_{\text{TI}}
(models
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
and
e
), and otherwise
(model
f
)
a high-accuracy estimate obtained using VUMPS with a much larger MPS bond dimension
200
; note that in the latter case, the deviation of our relaxations from the true energy density is strictly
smaller
than the error
\Delta E
shown in
Fig.

4
(due to the variational nature of the obtained energy density). In addition, for comparison
we also provide the
distance
\Delta{E}^{\text{var.}}(D)
of the energy of the variationally optimized MPS which we employed for the coarse-graining, i.e. the precision of the corresponding
upper bound
; it is shown in the column to the right of each panel.
(Note that the MPS data for models
(a)
and
(b)
has already been shown in
Fig.

1
.)

The first feature observed in all plots is the linear decay (on a log-log-scale) of the LTI energy (black line with circles),
\Delta E_{\text{LTI}}(n)\propto n^{-\alpha}
, with an exponent
\alpha\approx 2
throughout all models. The MPS curves

\Delta{E}_{\text{MPS}}^{\text{relax.}}(n,D)
for different
D
(colored dotted and dash-dotted lines) initially follow the (extrapolated) LTI line, and eventually flatten out and converge to a constant value

\Delta{E}_{\text{MPS}}^{\text{relax.}}(n=\infty,D)

as
n
gets larger. As
D
is increased, the LTI slope is followed for longer, and the attainable energy is improved.

A closer inspection of this behavior for the gapped models
c
and
e
reveals that the
curves obtained from our relaxation in fact fall below the linear extrapolation of the LTI line (whether for even or odd
n
);
the same behavior was observed for the TFI model in its gapped phase (not shown).
Since
\Delta E^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{MPS}}(n,D)\geq\Delta E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
,
this shows that the LTI energy actually converges super-algebraically (or at least with a larger slope) in gapped models.
This improved convergence rate could not have been inferred from the LTI results themselves, as they are limited to small
n
, but was only possible using the data obtained from our relaxation.

In all the critical models (all but
\mathbf{c}
and
\mathbf{e}
), the line traced by the tightest result obtained with our relaxation for each
n
coincides with the linear extrapolation of the LTI results in the log-log-plot. (The relaxation results were computed for even values of
n
, and the slope obtained from a linear fit to those points lies between the even-
n
and odd-
n
LTI slopes.)
From this extrapolation, we can determine the effective LTI problem size
n
,
n_{\text{eff.}}(D)
, required to obtain the same accuracy as the one achievable with a given
D

\Delta{E}_{\text{MPS}}^{\text{relax.}}(n=\infty,D)
.
We find that for the critical TFI model (
a
)
n_{\text{eff.}}(6)\simeq 120
, while for the Heisenberg model (
b
)
n_{\text{eff.}}(7)\simeq 60
, and for models
d
and
f

n_{\text{eff.}}(7)\simeq 40
. In all cases, this lies significantly above the
n
reachable for the LTI problem, whose complexity grows exponentially in
n
. More generally, the effective
n
exhibits an algebraic scaling

n_{\text{eff.}}(D)\propto D^{\kappa}
,
with a slope
\kappa
consistent with the well-known correlation length vs. bond dimension scaling for MPS,

\kappa=6/(c(\sqrt{12/c}+1))

(with
c
the central charge)
Tagliacozzo et al. 2008
;
Pirvu et al. 2012
;
Stojevic et al. 2015
;
Pollmann et al. 2009
. Together with the
n^{-2}
scaling of the LTI accuracy, this gives a scaling prediction
\Delta E^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{MPS}}\propto D^{-2\kappa}
attainable with the MPS-based relaxation for a given
D
.

A remarkable feature is that the distance of the lower bounds from the true energy
\Delta{E}_{\text{MPS}}^{\text{relax.}}(n=\infty,D)
is generally comparable to the upper bounds obtained from the variationally optimized MPS
\Delta{E}^{\text{var.}}(D)
, and in some cases even better by up to half an order. This is rather surprising, given that the MPS has been explicitly optimized to give the optimal upper bound, while we have simply used the same MPS for the coarse-graining for the lower bound, without optimizing over the MPS in any way to obtain optimal lower bounds. This seems to hint that a good renormalization-based variational ansatz also forms a good basis for a coarse-graining relaxation, a connection that deserves further study.
Motivated by this, we have carried out additional numerical studies on the
correlation between the precision of the lower and upper bounds, on which we report in

Section

VI.4
below.

Figure 4:

The error of the lower bounds to the ground-state energies of the models listed in
Table

1
obtained by solving the MPS-based and tree-tensor-network (TTN) based relaxations,
Eqs.

35
and
39
, of the translation-invariant (TI) local Hamiltonian problem
Eq.

1
.
Both methods are relaxations of the LTI problem,
Eq.

5
.
The error of the LTI lower bound,

\Delta{E}_{\text{LTI}}(n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{\text{TI}}-E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
, is plotted by the solid black line with circles.
The dotted and dash-dotted lines plot
\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{MPS}}(n,D)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{\text{TI}}-\hat{E}_{\text{MPS}}^{(D)}(n)

obtained from the MPS-based implementation, and the solid blue lines
correspond to the tree-tensor-network-based variant and plot
\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{TTN}}(n,D)
, both of which are shown for various sizes
n
and various bond dimensions
D
.
In the tree-tensor-network-based method the coarse-graining maps were optimized numerically as described in
Section

V.2
.
The bar to the right of each panel shows the precision of the variational
upper bound
,
\Delta{E}^{\text{var.}}
, corresponding to the MPS used in the MPS-based relaxation.

In addition,
Fig.

4
reports data obtained using the tree-tensor-network-based method for different
D
(blue lines).
The tree-tensor-network-based variant did not perform as well as the MPS-based one; nevertheless,
it improved upon the precision attainable by solving the LTI problem.
That this variant performed less well than the MPS-based one was partly to be expected because
the tree-tensor-network-based method is a relaxation of
Eq.

36
, which by itself is already a relaxation of the LTI problem.
In this variant, we have included the optimization over the coarse-graining maps described in
Section

V.2
;
the data reported in
Fig.

4
is the optimized data.
Through this optimization, we were able to decrease the error of the lower bound by factors ranging from 2 to 6. (E.g., in the TFI model with
D=3
and
n=64
the error was
\Delta{E}^{\text{relax.}}_{\text{TTN}}(64,3)=0.0006
prior to optimizing, and
0.000096
afterwards.)
Let us note that the way we construct the initial CPTP maps from the tree tensors for this relaxation
is ad hoc and could certainly be improved. Alternatively, one could pursue alternative methods to optimize over the coarse-graining maps, such as
gradient-decent methods obtained by differentiating the SDP solution with respect to the entries of the tensors as described in Refs.
Agrawal et al. 2019
;
Reehorst et al. 2021
; such methods would not require the coarse-graining maps to be CPTP maps, and should thus directly apply to general coarse-graining schemes.

A feature which merits discussion are the even-odd steps displayed both by the LTI energy and its approximation through the MPS relaxation for all models but the Ising model. All those models have antiferromagnetic couplings, suggesting that the origin of those steps could be traced back to magnetic frustration occurring in XXZ rings

Karbach and Mutter 1995
;
Marić et al. 2020
;
Li and He 2019
.
To understand this, note that the LTI problem on
n
sites can be obtained from the ground state
problem on any periodic chain of size
m\geq n
by omitting some constraints: The LTI energy density
E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
is thus upper bounded by the smallest ground state energy density for any ring of size
m\geq n
,
\min_{m\geq n}E_{\text{PBC}}(m)
.
For the models
(b)
to
(f)
, the ground state energy density of the periodic chain shows an oscillatory behavior, with the odd-
m
energy density being higher due to frustration. Thus, the upper bound

\min_{m\geq n}E_{\text{PBC}}(m)
shows precisely the same step-like behavior as observed for
E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
in
Fig.

4
. It is thus plausible that the step-like behavior of
E_{\text{LTI}}(n)
has the same origin. Remarkably, we find that for
models
\mathbf{b}
through
\mathbf{f}
, as well as in all spin-
1/2
XXZ models with
\Delta\in[-2,2]
,

E_{\text{LTI}}(n)=\min_{m\geq n}E_{\text{PBC}}(m)
(up to solver precision), i.e., the
the solution of the LTI problem
for even
n
is in fact
equal
to
E_{\text{PBC}}(n)
. This is certainly remarkable, as the LTI problem is a relaxation of the PBC problem, and deserves further study. Let us note that this equality, however, breaks down for more general models (e.g. in the XYZ family), and thus must be rooted in some special properties of the models considered.

Figure 5:

The precision of the tightest lower bound obtained for a given memory budget (the number of variables in the problem) by solving the MPS-based relaxation
Eq.

35
(solid lines), as well as the LTI problem
Eq.

5
(dotted lines) for the models listed in
Table

1
. The LTI lines follow a logarithmic curves whereas the MPS-relaxation data follow linear trends. The slopes of the lines were found to be between
-0.59
and
-0.74
depending on the model.

VI.3
Efficiency Analysis

Let us now assess the performance of the method, that is,
the way in which the resources required to solve the problem scale with the precision of the lower bound.
To this end, we compare in
Fig.

5
the
improvement in precision as a function of the number of variables in the problem,
N_{\text{vars.}}
,
for the MPS-based relaxation [
Eq.

35
] and the exact LTI problem [
Eq.

5
],
using the entire data set obtained for all the models (see
Table

1
) on which we tested our method.
In
Fig.

5
,
the dotted lines represent the LTI problem and the solid lines our MPS-based relaxation,
for each of the models
a
through
f
(shown in different colors).
We observe that LTI lines become increasingly flat; indeed, from the exponential scaling

N_{\text{vars.}}(\text{LTI(n)})\propto d^{2n}
, together with the observed
\Delta E_{\text{LTI}}(n)\propto n^{-\alpha}
,
we expect it to scale as
-\log(\,\cdot\,)
in the log-log-plot. Our relaxation results, on the other hand, seems to follow a clear linear trend in the plot.
This demonstrates that our method results in algebraic convergence of the energy as a function of the size of the SDP. The observed rates of convergence for the different models scale as
N^{-\beta}_{\text{vars.}}
with
\beta\in[0.59,0.74]
, depending on the model;
this is in line with the different exponents for
n_{\text{eff}}(D)\propto D^{\kappa}
and the
n^{-2}
scaling of the energy accuracy.

VI.4
Correlation Between Upper and Lower Bounds

Figure 6:

Precision of the lower bound obtained by solving the MPS-based relaxation
Eq.

35
as a function of the precision of the variational upper bound corresponding to the MPS that was used to construct the coarse-graining maps for the relaxation. For each
i\in\{0,1,2,4,8,\ldots,64\}
300 random MPS tensors with bond dimension
D
were generated and updated by
i
iterations of the VUMPS algorithm. Each tensor was then used to obtain a lower bound using our relaxation scheme, and an upper bound by evaluating the energy density of the uniform MPS generated by the tensor.

A remarkable observation was how well variationally optimized MPS performed when used as coarse-graining maps for our relaxation, resulting in lower bounds of comparable precision. To further investigate this issue, we have numerically studied the relation between the quality of the upper and lower bounds obtained from a given MPS.
To this end, we have generated random MPS tensors and applied the VUMPS algorithm to them for a number of iterations between
0
and
64
; this way, we could sample MPS which provide variational upper bounds of increasing accuracy.
The resulting data is shown in
Fig.

6
.
For each tensor, the upper bound was obtained by evaluating the energy density of the corresponding MPS; its distance from the ground state energy,
\Delta{E}_{\text{VUMPS}}
, is shown on the
x
-axis.
The lower bound for each tensor was obtained by solving the MPS-based relaxation,
Eq.

35
, using the coarse-graining maps constructed from the given MPS. The corresponding error

\Delta{E}_{\text{relax.}}
of the lower bound is shown on the
y
-axis.

Figure

6
shows a clear correlation between the precision of the upper and the lower bounds.
It also shows that while the variationally obtained MPS does not always give rise
to the tightest lower bound attainable with that bond dimension,
the difference between the best lower bound and that obtained from the MPS variationally optimized with VUMPS for a given bond dimension
is small when compared to the improvement obtained when going to a larger bond dimension.

VI.5
Numerical implementation

We conclude this section with some details regarding our numerical implementations. We used predominantly general-purpose software packages to solve the SDPs involved. We used YALMIP
Löfberg 2004
for modeling the problems and solved primarily using MOSEK
ApS 2019
. The unfavorable memory scaling of interior-point methods (see e.g.
Yurtsever et al. 2021
) prevented us from going to bond dimensions higher than 7 in the MPS-based variant and 5 in the tree-tensor-network-based variant. To overcome this, we tried to use the splitting conic solver (SCS)
O’Donoghue et al. 2016
which is a first-order method and requires less memory, but observed slow convergence and were not able to improve the results in any of the models, with the exception of the
S=1
Heisenberg chain. In this model we used our own matrix-free implementation of SCS (where the constraint matrix is not explicitly constructed as a sparse matrix, but only implicitly applied, which provides significant savings due to the tensor-network structure of the constraints) to produce the results for bond dimensions 6 through 9. In addition, SCS was used to solve the larger instances of the LTI problem,
Eq.

5
, where it performed very well.
We have not explored further methods beyond the ones mentioned. There could well be SDP algorithms out there that are better suited for our problem. We believe the results we presented could be markedly improved upon by using custom-tailored solvers. Matrix-free solvers could be especially suitable because of the special form of the constraints in our relaxation.

VII
Extensions of the method

In this section, we outline several extensions to the method: First, how to improve its performance by accounting for symmetries;
second, how to extend it to models beyond 1D chains, such as to two-dimensional spin systems, as well as
to more general scenarios using reduced-density-matrix theory; and third, how to apply it to entanglement and non-locality detection.

VII.1
Accounting for Symmetries

If the Hamiltonian has a symmetry we can simplify the ground state energy problem by restricting the search to symmetric states. Such states can be represented with fewer variables than general states that do not observe the symmetry.
In
Section

IV
we already used translation symmetry when formulating the relaxation of the translation-invariant problem by expressing everything in terms of one state of each size.

Hamiltonians of interest often possess further symmetries. Consider a Hamiltonian which is invariant under the action of a group
G
simultaneously on all spins:
[U_{g},H]=0
, where
U_{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\otimes_{i}u_{g})
and
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G\mapsto{\cal L}({\cal H})
is a unitary group representation of
G
on the local spin Hilbert space
{\cal H}
.
We will now describe how one can incorporate such symmetries in our relaxation scheme.

Consider the MPS-based relaxation scheme in the 1D setting,
Eq.

35
. In this case we can construct coarse-graining maps from symmetric MPS. Symmetric MPS have the property that they intertwine between the representation
u_{g}
on the physical Hilbert space and a representation on the virtual degrees of freedom (
v_{g}\otimes\overline{v}_{g}
)
Pérez-García et al. 2008
. Now consider for example the symmetry constraint on a
4
-body state
\rho_{\{1,2,3,4\}}
. When we compress this variable using a coarse-graining map constructed out of a symmetric MPS we can use the intertwining property of the MPS tensor to obtain a symmetry condition for the compressed variable
\omega_{\{1,o^{l},o^{r},4\}}
as shown in
Fig.

7
. This means that we can formulate our relaxation in term of symmetric states which can have significantly less free parameters than general states.
One could similarly use symmetric tree tensor networks
Gunst et al. 2019
when formulating
Eq.

39
.

Figure 7:
Optimization over symmetric states can be relaxed in terms of symmetric coarse-grained variables.
The black circles in the diagram represent symmetric MPS tensors which intertwine between the group action on the physical spins,
u_{g}
(white circles), and the virtual representations on the bond degrees of freedom,
v_{g}
and
\overline{v}_{g}
(white squares).

VII.2
2D Models

Our applications of the method in the 1D setting suggest straightforward generalizations to 2D. Where we used MPS as a coarse-graining ansatz one could use their generalization to 2D—projected entangled-pair states (PEPS)—and for tree tensor networks one could simply use their 2D version
Tagliacozzo et al. 2009
.
The PEPS variant would however quickly run into the following problem: As we coarse-grain larger and larger regions
R
by contracting more PEPS tensors, the coarse-graining dimension grows as

D^{|\partial R|}
, where
|\partial R|
is the length of the boundary of
R
. This means that after applying the coarse-graining procedure we would still end up with a problem that grows exponentially in size as the region
R
is increased.

The tree-tensor-network variant of the method does not suffer from this drawback. The construction we presented for the 1D case can be directly applied in the setting of a square lattice if in
Eq.

36
we replace the states supported on chains of spins
\{1,\ldots,2^{m}\}
with ones supported on squares
\{(i,j)\mid i,j=1,\ldots,2^{m}\}
. This would require working with states of size
D^{16}
which is prohibitive. However, by exploiting local symmetries as described in the previous paragraph one might be able to bring the problem down to a tractable size. Less resource-demanding coarse-graining schemes could be devised using trees of different geometries.

VII.3
Going Beyond State Ensembles: Reduced-Density-Matrix Theory

VII.3.1
Relaxation Using Moment Matrices

Reduced-Density-Matrix Theory (RDMT) can be regarded as a generalization of optimizations over state ensembles
Nakata et al. 2008
;
Mazziotti and Rice 2007
;
Baumgratz and Plenio 2012
. It is based on the notion of
moment matrices
, which we now introduce.
Let
\bm{X}
be a tuple of operators with
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\bm{X}\right\rvert}
elements:
\bm{X}=[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{\mid\bm{X}\mid}]
. The moment matrix of an operator
O
with respect to
\bm{X}
is defined as

\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X},O}_{i,j}=\mbox{Tr}(OX_{i}^{\dagger}X_{j}),\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\bm{X}\right\rvert}\}\;.

(43)

We say that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}
is a moment matrix (with respect to
\bm{X}
) if there exists an operator
O
such that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}=\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X},O}
, and denote the set of all such matrices by
{\cal M}^{2}_{\bm{X}}
.

Let
\bm{v}=[v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\mid\bm{X}\mid}]^{\intercal}
be a vector of complex numbers
v_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
and
V=\sum_{i}v_{i}X_{i}
an operator.
From

\bm{v}^{\dagger}\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X},O}\bm{v}=\mbox{Tr}(OV^{\dagger}V)

it follows that for any
\bm{X}
and
O\geq 0
it holds that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X},O}\geq 0
. Furthermore if the set
\bm{X}
contains a full basis of operators then
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X},O}\geq 0
implies
O\geq 0
.
I.e. when
\bm{X}
is a basis, there is a one-to-one correspondence between positive semidefinite operators and positive semidefinite moment matrices with respect to
\bm{X}
.

Let
\bm{X}
consist of a basis of operators including the identity
X_{1}={\mathbb{I}}
.
We can express the Hamiltonian in that basis as
H=\sum_{i}h_{i}X_{i}
and, according to the above correspondence, rewrite the Hamiltonian problem in terms of moment matrices.

\displaystyle\min_{\rho}

\displaystyle\mbox{Tr}\left(H\rho\right)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{\Gamma^{X}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i}h_{i}\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}_{1,i}

(44)

\displaystyle\mbox{s.t. }

\displaystyle\rho\geq 0,

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mbox{s.t. }

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}\in{\cal M}^{2}_{\bm{X}},\;\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\mbox{Tr}(\rho)=1

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}_{1,1}=1\;.

We can now relax the problem by replacing the positivity constraint on
\bm{\Gamma^{X}}
with a weaker constraint that demands that only a sub-block of
\bm{\Gamma^{X}}
is positive semidefinite:
Let
\bm{Y}\subset\bm{X}
contain enough operators from
\bm{X}
such that we can still expand the Hamiltonian in terms of its elements
H=\sum_{i}\tilde{h}_{i}Y_{i}
, and let
Y_{1}={\mathbb{I}}
.
Demanding that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}
is a positive semidefinite moment matrix is equivalent to demanding that there exists an operator
O
that is not necessarily positive semidefinite on the whole Hilbert space, but only on a subspace (
\mbox{Tr}(OV^{\dagger}V)\geq 0
for all
V
in the span of
\{Y_{i}\}
).
The following is therefore a relaxation of the Hamiltonian problem:

\begin{split}\min_{\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}}&\sum_{i}\tilde{h}_{i}\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}_{1,i}\\
\mbox{s.t.\ }&\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}\in{\cal M}^{2}_{\bm{Y}},\;\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}\geq 0,\;\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}_{1,1}=1\;.\end{split}

(45)

The condition
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}\in{\cal M}^{2}_{\bm{Y}}
amounts to linear constraints between the entries of
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}
which can be computed directly from the operators
\{Y_{i}\}
: whenever there are linear dependencies between the operators
\{S_{i,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=Y_{i}^{\dagger}Y_{j}\}_{i,j}
, the entries of
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}
must have the same dependencies. This ensures that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}_{i,j}
are given by the action of a linear functional on
Y_{i}^{\dagger}Y_{j}
, i.e. that there exists an operator
O
such that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}}=\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y},O}
.

Equation

45
is thus an SDP involving a matrix of size
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\bm{Y}\right\rvert}\times\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\bm{Y}\right\rvert}
.

As an example consider a local Hamiltonian on a finite spin chain of length
n
and
\bm{Y}
consisting of all operators of the form

{\mathbb{I}}^{\otimes l}\otimes\sigma_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\sigma_{i_{k}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}^{\otimes n-l-k}
, where
\{\sigma_{i}\}
form a basis of single spin operators which includes the identity.
In this case the constraints in
Eq.

45
demand that there exists an operator
O
,
which for any
k
-body operator
V
satisfies

\mbox{Tr}(O{\mathbb{I}}^{\otimes l}\otimes V_{{l+1},\ldots,{l+k}}^{\dagger}V_{{l+1},\ldots,{l+k}}\otimes{\mathbb{I}}^{\otimes n-l-k})=\mbox{Tr}\left(\mbox{Tr}_{\{1,\ldots,l,l+k+1,\ldots,n\}}(O)V^{\dagger}V\right)\geq 0\;.

(46)

I.e. with this choice of
\bm{Y}
,
Eq.

45
includes all the constraints that would be imposed if we optimized over an ensemble of mutually compatible
k
-body states
\{\rho_{\{l+1,\ldots,l+k\}}\}_{l}
, as those arise as reduced states of
O
.

VII.3.2
Applications of Reduced-Density-Matrix Theory

This relaxation method is best known for its application in quantum chemistry where it has been used to accurately compute the shape of the potential-energy curve of small molecules
Nakata et al. 2001
;
Mazziotti and Rice 2007
.
Since the electronic Hamiltonian includes only 2-body interactions, the energy can be evaluated from the 2-body reduced density matrix.
The moment matrix
\bm{\Gamma}
is formed with respect to all products of
p
creation and annihilation operators
a_{i},a^{\dagger}_{j}
, where the indices
i,j
correspond to a finite set of single-electron wavefunctions. Constraining
\bm{\Gamma}
to be positive semidefinite imposes the so called
p
-positivity conditions on the 2-body reduced density matrix
Mazziotti and Erdahl 2001
.
By increasing
p
one obtains tighter lower bounds on the minimum eigenvalue of the model Hamiltonian (the restriction of the full Hamiltonian to the space of
N
-electron wavefunctions spanned by
\{a^{\dagger}_{j}\}
).
In practice conditions beyond
p=3
are rarely implemented because even the most resource efficient SDP solvers reach their limits
Mazziotti 2011
;
Nakata et al. 2008
;
Zhao et al. 2004
.

The same method has also been applied to lattice systems and has appeared in the literature under several different names.
We shall now give a brief overview of related works, and compare their result with ours where relevant.
Ref.
Baumgratz and Plenio 2012
gives lower bounds for translation-invariant spin Hamiltonians: for the spin
1/2
Heisenberg model on periodic chains of sizes up to 50 they obtained energy densities
10^{-2}
below the true infinite-system value. The error of the bound we obtained for this system is
2\times 10^{-4}
.
In Ref.
Barthel and Hübener 2012
translation-invariant XXZ models on infinite lattices in 1D and 2D are treated.
The errors of the bounds they obtained for
H^{1/2}_{\text{XXZ}}(\Delta)
are
2\times 10^{-3}
for
\Delta=1
;
3\times 10^{-3}
for
\Delta=2
; and below
10^{-4}
for
\Delta=0
. We obtained
3\times 10^{-4}
,
2\times 10^{-4}
, and
3\times 10^{-4}
respectively.
In Ref.
Haim et al. 2020
a relaxation based on something very similar to moment matrices is applied to finite periodic spin chains. For the critical Ising model and XXZ models with
\Delta\in[0,2]
they obtained errors of the order
10^{-2}
and
10^{-1}
respectively.
Refs.
Hammond and Mazziotti 2006
;
Shenvi and Izmaylov 2010
treat the Hubbard model in 1D and Refs.
Barthel and Hübener 2012
;
Han 2020
also in 2D.
Moment matrices also appear as the main object in a recently introduced relaxation framework known as the bootstrap approach
Lin 2020
;
Han et al. 2020
;
Berenstein and Hulsey 2021
.

RDMT offers some flexibility in formulating relaxations due to the freedom one has in choosing the set of operators
\bm{Y}
. An example of this can be seen in Ref.
Barthel and Hübener 2012
where the set
\bm{Y}
consists of products of single-site creation and annihilation operators.
Operators of degree
k
are included in
\bm{Y}
only if all of their
k
creation and annihilation operators act within a region
\Omega_{k}
. By making
\Omega_{k}
smaller for larger
k
(up to
\Omega_{k}=\varnothing
for all
k>k_{\text{max}}
), the size of the optimization problem is controlled while prioritizing local operators of high degree.
A similar locality criterion is used in Ref.
Han 2020
.
Within this approach one could optimize the choice of the regions
\Omega_{k}
depending on the given Hamiltonian (in analogy to the ideas proposed in Ref.
Requena et al. 2021
for state ensembles).
However, as we have demonstrated in this paper, a vast number of constraints can be further relaxed
without compromising the precision of the energy for a given target Hamiltonian. We will now outline how our relaxation procedure can be implemented within the RDMT framework.

VII.3.3
Extending Reduced-Density-Matrix Theory Through Renormalization

We can generalize the relaxation scheme described in
Sections

II
and
III
to accommodate for representations of states in terms of moment matrices.
We start by writing the exact problem in terms of a moment matrix
\bm{\Gamma^{X}}
with respect to a full basis of operators
\bm{X}
, as we have done in
Eq.

44
above.
We assume that we can pick
\bm{X}
such that the Hamiltonian can be expressed in terms of a small subset
\bm{Y}_{0}\subset\bm{X}
(as in the case of a basis consisting of strings of single-spin operators and a local Hamiltonian).
The Hamiltonian problem can then be written as

\begin{split}\min_{\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}},\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}}&\sum_{i}\tilde{h}_{i}\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}_{1,i}\\
\mbox{s.t.\ }&\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}\in{\cal M}^{2}_{\bm{X}},\;\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}\geq 0\\
&\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}_{1,1}=1\\
&\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}\leftarrow\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}\;,\end{split}

(47)

where by
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}\leftarrow\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}
we denoted the requirement that
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}
appears as a block in
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}
in the appropriate positions.

Similarly to the way we treated the constraint
\rho^{(2)}\leftarrow\psi
in
Section

II
, we expand the constraint
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}\leftarrow\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}
in
Eq.

47
in terms of a hierarchy of constraints between moment matrices of increasing sizes
\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{0}}\leftarrow\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{Y}_{1}}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow\bm{\Gamma}^{\bm{X}}
corresponding to operator tuples
\bm{Y}_{0}\subset\bm{Y}_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\bm{X}
.
Next, a coarse-graining procedure which is compatible with the sequence
\bm{Y}_{0}\subset\bm{Y}_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\bm{X}
in the sense of
Fig.

2
has to be constructed. The coarse-graining maps could be chosen such that they map moment matrices to moment matrices.
The relaxation is finally obtained analogously to the procedure described in
Section

III
, by representing the coarse-grained moment matrices in terms of compressed moment matrices.
It would be interesting to see if one could use this method to improve RDMT and bootstrap calculations.

VII.4
Entanglement and Nonlocality Detection

The general method described in
III.3
also leads to sound relaxations when the variables
\mathrm{P}_{m},\Omega_{m}
and the transformations
\mbox{Tr}_{\{\cdot\}},C
are interpreted not as representations of many-body quantum states and transformations thereof, but as representations of the many-body states of generalized probabilistic theories (GPTs)
Hardy 2001
;
Dakic and Brukner 2009
;
Barnum et al. 2014
;
Chiribella et al. 2010
and maps transforming such states.
As observed in
Navascués et al. 2020
, key problems in quantum information theory, such as entanglement and nonlocality detection in many-body systems, can be interpreted as determining the existence of a state in a GPT.

The formalism of GPTs was introduced to describe and reason about physical theories of all conceivable sorts: quantum, classical or else. A GPT is specified by a list of physical system types, together with composition rules that detail the type one must use to describe composite systems.
In quantum mechanics, system types are specified by the system’s Hilbert space dimension, and the tensor composition rule tells us that the type of a composite system consisting of a subsystem with dimension
d_{1}
and a subsystem with dimension
d_{2}
is
d_{1}d_{2}
. Each system type
T
comes with a
state space

{\cal S}_{T}
, namely, a convex set of vectors, each of which describes a possible state of system
T
. The composition rule must also specify how to derive the marginals of a composite system, given a description of its overall state, as well as how to represent independent system preparations in a joint system description. Transformations in a GPT correspond to linear maps that, acting on part of the state of a bipartite system, always return a valid bipartite state. That is, the linear map
M
, transforming systems of type
T
into systems of type
T^{\prime}
, is physical if, for all system types
U
, and all states
s_{UT}\in{\cal S}_{UT}
,
({\mathbb{I}}_{U}\otimes M_{T})(s_{UT})\in{\cal S}_{UT^{\prime}}
. In
Navascués et al. 2020
such transformations are called
connectors
.
In the case of quantum theory this is just the complete positivity condition.

Consider for example the
entanglement marginal problem

Navascués et al. 2021
, i.e., deciding if the observed state ensemble
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
of a many-body system corresponds to the marginals of a fully separable quantum state. This is equivalent to deciding if
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
are the marginals of a global state in the GPT
SEP
defined in
Navascués et al. 2020
, whose state spaces are, precisely, the sets of fully separable quantum states.
More generally, the
GPT marginal problem
would ask if the ensemble of GPT states
\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}}
arises as marginalizations of a global GPT state
\rho_{{\cal A}}
. The dual of this problem can be called the
GPT local Hamiltonian problem
:

\displaystyle\min H(\{\rho_{I}\}_{I}),

s.t.

\displaystyle\{\rho_{I}\}_{I\in{\cal I}},\mbox{ admit a GPT state extension}.

(48)

Finding a tight lower bound to
Eq.

48
then allows to use the Hamiltonian H as a witness for the presence of the property of interest, e.g. entanglement.
(In the usual marginal problem setting the detected property is
incompatibility
with a global state: An energy value lower than the ground state energy indicates that the state ensemble with that energy is incompatible with a global state.)

Clearly,
Eq.

27
corresponds to a lower bound on the GPT local Hamiltonian problem,
Eq.

48
, if we regard
\mathrm{P}_{0},\Omega_{m}
as ensembles of states of a GPT;
\mbox{Tr}_{\{\cdot\}}
as the marginalization map provided by the GPT’s composition rule;
B_{m}
as connectors; and the sets
S_{{\cal J}_{m}}
as the direct sum of the GPT state spaces corresponding to each of the types
J\in{\cal J}_{m}
.

VIII
Conclusion

In this paper, we have introduced a general method for obtaining rigorous
lower bounds for a wide range of minimization problems in a quantum
many-body setting. As a prime application, we demonstrated how our method
can be applied to obtain rigorous lower bounds to the ground state energy of
quantum spin systems. At the same time, the method lends itself to a wide
range of other optimization problems with a similar structure, such as the
certification of entanglement and non-locality in quantum information
theory. The method achieves this by providing a systematic and
customizable way to obtain outer approximations to the set of physical
quantum correlations; it is thus suggestive that it can also provide new
ideas on how to relax other numerical methods based on positivity constraints,
such as reduced-density-matrix theory or various bootstrap techniques.

The key idea of our method is to relax the convex set of few-body density
matrices or other correlations which are physical—that is, compatible
with a global quantum state—in two steps. In a first step, the global
compatibility constraint is decomposed into a hierarchy of constraints
between increasingly complex objects. A first relaxation can then be
obtained by truncating the hierarchy at a finite level
n
. Approaches of this
kind have been pursued previously in various settings; however, they
suffer from the exponential growth of the involved objects, which severely
limits the level of the hierarchy that one can reach.

The key novelty of our method lies in the second relaxation: We apply a
coarse-graining procedure to all objects in the hierarchy, in a way which
compresses them down to objects of a fixed (or otherwise tractable) size.
This compression must be chosen such as to keep the relevant degrees of
freedom, which can be accomplished, e.g., by employing
renormalization-based ideas. At the same time, any such compression
scheme must act in a consistent way across all levels of the hierarchy, a
demand yet again met by renormalization-based coarse-graining maps.

By utilizing this compression, we can effectively reach very large levels

n
of the original hierarchy, and thus achieve results with a much
improved accuracy. At the same time, the optimization problem remains a
semidefinite program (SDP), and can thus be efficiently solved. In
addition, the possibility to smoothly adjust the coarse-graining maps to
best suit the problem at hand, and to optimize them as well in the course of the
optimization, makes our relaxation much more tunable than existing
approaches, which are based on discrete, and thus rigid, hierarchies. Moreover,
insights from renormalization procedures can be used as a guide in
choosing those maps.

We have worked out our method in detail for 1D quantum spin chains, and
demonstrated its power through the explicit numerical study of a range of
relevant models. Here, we have employed two coarse-graining schemes, both
of which are motivated by renormalization-based variational ansatzes: One
based on Matrix Product States (MPS), and one on Tree Tensor Networks
(TTN); in the latter case, we have additionally implemented an
optimization over the coarse-grainers. In both cases, we observed that the
variationally optimized wavefunction also provides a very good
coarse-graining map for the relaxation of the hierarchy, in line with the
intuition that the coarse-graining should keep low-energy degrees of
freedom, akin to renormalization schemes. We have tested both approaches
on a wide range of gapped as well as critical models. We found that both
schemes clearly outperform the exact truncated hierarchy, with the MPS
approach generally performing noticeably better. Specifically, using the
MPS-based relaxation, we were able to gain between one and two orders of
magnitude in precision in energy, on par with sizes of the truncated
hierarchy between
n\approx 40
and
n\approx 120
(while the
exact hierarchy was limited to
n=13
). Overall, we observed that the
resources required for our method scaled polynomially in the targeted
energy precision, as opposed to the exponential scaling of the exact
truncated hierarchy.

The general framework presented in this paper can be applied to a much
broader range of problems than those that we used as a demonstration.
First, the method opens avenues to obtain provable lower bounds on ground
state energies for two-dimensional systems, where good variational upper
bounds e.g. using tensor networks are much harder to obtain, and where
the performance of those methods is less well understood. It can also be
applied to reduced-density-matrix theory and related approaches based on
correlation matrices, and thus to problems without an underlying locality
notion, such as in quantum chemistry. Finally, at its heart, our
relaxation presents a way to certify when a (very large or even infinite)
matrix that satisfies a set of linear and convex constraints on its
entries (such as having certain two-body marginals or few-point
correlations, or being a separable state) cannot be chosen to be positive;
as such, it is also applicable to problems such as entanglement or
non-locality detection, as well as to the so-called bootstrap approach to
many-body problems.

A key challenge in generalizing our method is the right choice of coarse-graining maps. In this regard, we have demonstrated that maps based on tensor networks, which can in most cases be interpreted as variational wavefunctions underlying different renormalization schemes
Cirac and Verstraete 2009
, are a suitable option. It is reasonable to expect that tensor network ansatzes will work in higher dimensional problems as well. Moreover, given the often excellent performance of tensor networks beyond quantum lattice problems, it is likely that suitably chosen tensor network ansatzes will also make good coarse-grainers in scenarios other than energy minimization. Remarkably, tensor networks optimized to derive variational upper bounds generally performed surprisingly well when used as coarse-grainers in our relaxation scheme; a better understanding of this connection, as well as of the ways in which different tensor networks can be used as coarse-graining maps, remain as open problems for future work.

Tensor Networks: Mathematical Structures and Novel Algorithms
.

(Grant Agreement No. 863476),
